Microsoft barred from sponsoring foreign workers for US residency
2026-10-09

Microsoft barred from sponsoring foreign workers for US residency

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Donald Trump’s administration has temporarily banned Microsoft, Adobe, and several other tech companies from sponsoring workers for permanent US residency, claiming they abused immigration programs at the expense of American citizens. Vice-president JD Vance said on Thursday that the H-1B visa programme for highly skilled foreign workers had “become rife with fraud” and that over-reliance on such schemes was “a fundamental insult to the American worker.” He added that “no company in the US” had “abused this system” more than Microsoft. Vance’s message to Microsoft that it could not “lay off American workers and then replace them with foreign indentured servants” triggered an angry reaction from the Indian government. “Resorting to terminology that carries painful historical and colonial legacy connotations is deeply offensive,” the foreign ministry in New Delhi said in a statement on Friday. Referring to Vance’s remarks, it said “such descriptions are unwarranted and ignore the fact that Indian professionals in the United States are highly educated and skilled contributors to its economy and innovation ecosystem.” Six other IT outsourcing companies—Cognizant, Infosys, Tata, Wipro, HCL, and Capgemini—were also suspended from the Permanent Labor Certification, or Perm, program, which allows US employers to hire foreign workers on a permanent basis. “Subpoenas have been served, investigations are under way and nobody will be getting a free pass because their name is carved into an expensive building,” said Labor Department Inspector-General Anthony D’Esposito. Microsoft said the “vast majority” of its employees in the US were American, while Tata Consultancy Services, India’s largest outsourcing company, said its strategy was centred on hiring locally in the US and that Perm applications were in “single digits in the last two years.” Adobe and Tata as well as Cognizant, Infosys, Wipro, HCL, and Capgemini did not respond to requests for comment. The announcement marks the latest move in the Trump administration’s broad crackdown on immigration. It has previously taken aim at H-1B visas widely used by tech companies and specialist industries. Last year, the White House attempted to impose a $100,000 fee on new applications, but, in June, a federal judge ruled the fee amounted to an unauthorized tax and infringed on Congress’ authority to raise funds. The Trump administration has appealed against the ruling. Before the fee increase, the government charged $215 to register for the H-1B visa lottery and a $780 base fee for employers sponsoring visa applicants. More than 400,000 such visas were approved last year. While Indians have been the biggest recipients of H-1B visas and the new restrictions target many of the largest outsourcing groups, the impact is likely to be small as the route from H-1B to permanent residency has been out of reach for most Indians, given a decades-long backlog. Nearly 1 million people from India are waiting for employment-based permanent residency as of December last year, according to an August study by the non-profit National Foundation for American Policy. Nasscom, the Indian IT services lobbying group, said the number of employees transitioning to permanent residency through Perm was “relatively limited” as Indian tech companies had “significantly reduced their dependence on H-1B visas.” In his comments on Thursday, Vance said the visas were helping companies undercut US workers. He claimed some H-1B holders were earning between $20,000 and $48,000 less than US citizens hired for similar positions. Amazon, the biggest user of H-1B visas, was not mentioned in the press conference. But Vance singled out Microsoft, saying it “laid off last year 6,000 American workers at the same time the company benefited from 6,300 H-1B visas and almost 3,000 green cards,” referring to US permanent residency. The White House on Thursday also announced a fraud investigation into the use of J-1 student visas at nine American universities, including Harvard, Yale, and Stanford. Microsoft, whose shares dropped more than 1 percent following the announcement, said the visa applications mentioned by Vance were largely “to extend or change the status” of existing employees. “Microsoft only files H-1B petitions for those who meet the rigorous standards of this visa category,” the company said. “We pay our employees some of the highest compensation in the tech sector, and our wages are among the highest of all H-1B filings.” At a separate event on Thursday, the Trump administration gave Microsoft’s Indian-born chief executive, Satya Nadella, the National Medal of Technology and Innovation. He was honored alongside South African-born Elon Musk and Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang, who was born in Taiwan. Vance said there was no contradiction between the two announcements. “Our message to Microsoft is: ‘You’re a great American company, but you’ve got to hire great American workers.’” Additional reporting by Michael Stott in New Delhi © 2026 The Financial Times Ltd. All rights reserved. Not to be redistributed, copied, or modified in any way.

Rocket Report: A rare Falcon 9 scrub; is it just about Vulcan's time to shine?
2026-10-09

Rocket Report: A rare Falcon 9 scrub; is it just about Vulcan's time to shine?

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Welcome to Edition 9.14 of the Rocket Report! The big news this week, from our perspective, is that United Launch Alliance’s Vulcan rocket appears to be close to launching. We’ve seen a no-earlier-than launch date for the return to flight mission of October 29 (an Amazon Leo flight), and the next rocket up after that, also an Amazon Leo mission, is also planned for this year. Assuming the solid rocket boosters work, Vulcan could be poised for a major breakthrough next year in cadence. As always, we welcome reader submissions, and if you don’t want to miss an issue, please subscribe using the box below (the form will not appear on AMP-enabled versions of the site). Each report will include information on small-, medium-, and heavy-lift rockets as well as a quick look ahead at the next three launches on the calendar. Nuri rocket notches another success. South Korea successfully deployed five satellites for its first domestically launched microsatellite constellation during the fifth flight of its homegrown Nuri rocket Wednesday, Yonhap News Agency reports. In addition to the primary payload of five New-space Earth Observation Satellite Constellation for National Safety microsatellites, nine of the rocket’s 10 CubeSat secondary payloads were released as planned, according to the Korea AeroSpace Administration. PERSAT-02, developed by South Korean startup Quaternion, failed to separate from the rocket. Pushing for more commercial activity … The mission was Nuri’s fourth consecutive success after its debut flight failed in 2021, raising the small-lift rocket’s overall launch success rate to 80 percent. After the launch, Science Minister Bae Kyung-hoon pledged continued government support for additional launches led by the private sector as South Korea seeks to shift from a government-led space development model toward a more commercially driven industry. HyImpulse test flight slips into 2027. German launch startup HyImpulse has pushed the second flight of its SR75 suborbital rocket into spring 2027, European Spaceflight reports. As recently as last month, the company had said the flight would occur at the “end of 2026.” HyImpulse said the new target was based on its assessment of the remaining activities required under the license framework and the seasonal weather conditions at SaxaVord, rather than on the vehicle’s readiness. This will be the rocket’s first flight from European soil … The flight from SaxaVord will be HyImpulse’s second SR75 mission and its first to carry commercial payloads. The company completed an initial test flight of the suborbital rocket on May 3, 2024, from the Koonibba Test Range in South Australia. German launch company gets a new CEO. The Supervisory Board of Rocket Factory Augburg has appointed Alexander Dahm as CEO. He succeeds Indulis Kalnins and will be responsible for the company’s continued development and its path to the debut flight of the RFA ONE launch vehicle, the company said this week. Dahm has 30 years of aerospace experience, including involvement with the Ariane 5 program. Still seeking orbit … “I am impressed by the grit, innovative strength, and passion with which a new generation of European launch vehicles is being developed here. Together, we will reach the next important milestones on the way to the maiden flight of RFA ONE and actively shape the company’s future,” Dahm said. RFA has been working toward its first orbital launch following the loss of its first stage during a 2024 static-fire test. SpaceX has rare scrub of Falcon 9 late in countdown. A Falcon 9 launch planned for early Monday morning was scrubbed in the final minute of the count as the rocket was shifting into its automated startup sequence, Spaceflight Now reports. SpaceX didn’t provide a reason for the abort. The rocket is intended to launch a new batch of data satellites to low Earth orbit on behalf of the United States Space Force’s Space Development Agency. A relatively young booster … The company targeted Tuesday, October 6, for its next launch attempt, but waved off that opportunity as well. Following additional changes, the company is now aiming for no earlier than Saturday, October 10. The mission will use Falcon 9 first stage booster B1103. This will be its sixth flight following the launches of the SDA’s T1TL-E, NROL-179, and three batches of Starlink satellites. Europe’s progress on hop tests remains slow. The European Commission has extended the deadline for the SALTO reusable launcher project to the end of 2026, giving the program another three months to complete the first hop test of Europe’s Themis reusable rocket demonstrator, European Spaceflight reports. This is the latest in a series of extensions that have pushed the completion date back from the original November 2025 deadline. A long wait … The ArianeGroup-led SALTO initiative is an 42.8 million euro EU research project that is part of the ESA-managed Themis program. SALTO comprises the first two low-altitude hop tests, with the aim of demonstrating that Themis can be launched, recovered, and then launched again. Themis arrived at Esrange, Sweden, in June 2025, following a two-week road journey from ArianeGroup’s Les Mureaux facility in France. It has since been doing something important while not launching or hopping, we presume. Concerns about credit risks at SpaceX. SpaceX plans to raise $40 billion in debt to finance the purchase of Nvidia chips, and The Financial Times reports that this has raised concerns about the large company’s creditworthiness. The publication reports that the spread on SpaceX’s five-year credit default swaps rose to 194 basis points on Wednesday, meaning it now costs $19,400 annually to protect $1 million of its debt against default. Doubling down on Nvidia … As for the $40 billion in new debt, private capital firm Apollo was expected to lead the deal, while bond giant Pimco was among lenders in talks to fund it. It comes after the company issued $25 billion in investment-grade debt in June just weeks after raising $86 billion in its initial public offering. The new debt deal suggests SpaceX founder Elon Musk is doubling down on his bet on Nvidia’s technology. Cape Canaveral to get drone protection. Cape Canaveral Space Force Station is getting lasers that can zap drones intruding into its airspace, TWZ reports. When installed, the new directed energy defense system will be part of a much larger effort to protect the spaceport against drone incursions. The idea is to ensure launch safety and keep prying eyes away from an installation full of secrets about America’s military and commercial space efforts. Next step: sharks with laser beams? … Companies like SpaceX, United Launch Alliance, Blue Origin, and Relativity Space use the installation for space launches. The base defense modernization effort comes as the Cape Canaveral region is inundated with drone incursions. There were hundreds last year. For the spaceport, lasers are even more important than for other locations needing drone protection. That’s because the traditional method of jamming radio frequencies to interfere with drone connections to controllers could damage space vehicles. (submitted by biokleen) Vulcan stacking two launches for Amazon. In a wide-ranging interview with Ars, Amazon Leo officials discussed their progress toward delivering an orbital broadband service to customers back on Earth. Among the challenges faced by the company was launch. “From day one we decided to go with a diverse set of partners to supply us with rockets,” Amazon Leo VP Rajeev Badyal said. “We started off with ULA, Blue Origin, Arianespace, and SpaceX. We signed deals with all four of them. We knew there was risk, but we had to get heavy lift vehicles.” Vulcan on deck … Badyal said the company is currently working on back-to-back launches on Vulcan this year. “I can say we’re weeks away from launching on Vulcan,” he said. “Things are on track. We’ll follow that with an Arianespace launch and at least one more Vulcan this year. I can also tell you the infrastructure that we put and built with ULA now allows us to process two rockets simultaneously. So right now, there are two vehicle integration facilities, and both have a Vulcan rocket that is vertical and getting ready for our missions.” Blue Origin building three more pads. In an interview with Fox News this week, Blue Origin founder Jeff Bezos said the company was going to construct three more launch pads after the rebuild of LC-36A to accommodate New Glenn. “We’re building three more pads,” Bezos said. “So we’re building—we’ll have a total of four pads that we’re under construction right now.” Bezos said this in the context of the company’s recent request to the FAA for 50 launches a year from Florida. Bezos has much bigger goals long-term … “Our goal is to fly every day,” Bezos said. “And then once we’re flying every day, we want to fly every hour. And then once we’re flying every hour, we want to fly every minute. And by the way, that’s where I lose the team.” Bezos has long talked about making spaceflight routine, but launches every minute seems a tad ambitious for a company that has yet to launch once a month. October 9: Long March 12 | Unknown payload | Wenchang Space Launch Site, China | 19:25 UTC October 10: Falcon 9 | Tranche 1, Transport Layer A | Vandenberg Space Force Base, Calif. | 07:29 UTC October 12: Falcon 9 | Starlink 15-25 | Vandenberg Space Force Base, Calif. | 23:00 UTC

Volkswagen's replacement for the ID.4 crossover is here
2026-10-09

Volkswagen's replacement for the ID.4 crossover is here

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Volkswagen’s ID.4 is no more. The midsize crossover debuted in 2021, one of VW’s first new electric vehicles designed in the wake of dieselgate. A year later, the company began local production in Chattanooga, Tennessee, and a couple of years ago facelifted the model, adding a much-improved rear drive unit in the process. But Chattanooga turned its attention away from the ID.4 earlier this year. And, having sold almost a million ID.4s (and near-identical ID.5s) globally, the German automaker has decided it’s time for its replacement. Meet the ID. Tiguan. Dropping the numerical and adopting the moniker of VW’s big seller is a statement. “The Tiguan is our best-selling model worldwide, and the ID.4 and ID.5 are among our most successful electric cars. The ID. Tiguan brings these two success stories together,” said Thomas Schäfer, CEO of the VW brand. VW was an early pioneer of using flexible vehicle architectures to create a multitude of bodystyles and sizes with common parts, and its transition into building EVs hasn’t changed that. The ID. Tiguan uses an updated version of the company’s mass-market EV platform, now called MEB+, with either 58 kWh or 77 kWh battery packs. The smaller pack is only available with a 187 hp (140 kW) rear electric motor, but the 77 kWh battery can be had with either a 281 hp (210 kW) rear-wheel drive powertrain or as a 295 hp (220 kW) all-wheel drive version. Range is up to 373 miles (600 km), at least according to the European WLTP test. Alongside the mainstream name, it has a more mainstream look, though it’s part of VW’s new design language. For those keeping track of the various interesting names car companies give their design languages—it can’t just be me—this one is called Pure Positive. The automaker says it wants the ID. Tiguan to feel like it belongs to the premium rather than mainstream segment, both inside and out. Based on the images VW supplied to Ars, I think they’ve mostly met that brief; the R-Line interior in particular looks rather good to me. More importantly though, VW has been listening to feedback. The return to normal door handles will be welcome, even if it might have been driven by Chinese vehicle regulators. Even more welcome are the plethora of physical controls: real buttons on the steering wheel, real switches for the climate control and windows, and real physical air vents, not computer-controlled ones. There’s a new 15-inch infotainment screen that looks nice but will need to be responsive, plus the option of a 12-inch passenger display, a trend I continue to bemoan like Grandpa Simpson shouting at the clouds. One feature that really got my attention is one of the options for the new 10-inch digital main instrument display: the retro dials. Instead of something presumably 21st-century looking—I don’t know since we didn’t get any press shots of the default theme—you can opt to replicate the dials and warning lights from an Mk1 Golf, an Mk2 Golf Country, an Mk 4 Golf, a first-gen Tiguan, a racing Toureg, or the Beetle. (I’m not sure if it’s the original rear-engined Beetle or the retro one designed by J Mays in the late 1990s.) Pre-sales for the electric Tiguan start in Europe next week, with a starting price in Germany of 42,695 euros ($44,716). Although the ID. Tiguan is coming to the US, VW told us it “has not announced North American timing for the vehicle. Following the European ID. Tiguan launch in early 2027, the company will share North American timing, offer structure, pricing, and other market-specific details in due course.”

SpaceX calls for better coordination in orbit after near-misses with Starlink
2026-10-08

SpaceX calls for better coordination in orbit after near-misses with Starlink

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Space has plenty of room for more megaconstellations, but only if all operators are willing to share information about where their satellites are and where they’re going, a top executive at SpaceX and its AI subsidiary said this week. Michael Nicolls, SpaceX’s vice president for Starlink and president of Elon Musk’s artificial intelligence company, xAI, used a presentation Tuesday at the International Astronautical Congress in Turkey as a clarion call for improved data-sharing among satellite operators. SpaceX’s Starlink constellation now has more than 11,000 satellites, about two-thirds of all active spacecraft in low-Earth orbit. Radars and telescopes routinely track thousands more pieces of space junk. Starlink is, by far, the largest satellite constellation in operation today. But if you buy the buzz, Starlink is just the tip of the iceberg. SpaceX has revealed plans to launch up to a million satellites for a new constellation, called Starmind, to deploy AI computing capacity in low-Earth orbit. Amazon is in the early stages of launching a network of thousands of satellites to compete with Starlink, and two Chinese megaconstellations are now in full-scale deployment. All of this raises the question of how to mitigate the risk of a collision in orbit, an event that could create thousands more pieces of space junk, potentially triggering a self-perpetuating cycle of debris generation. The risk of a collision on any given day is fantastically low. Space is vast and empty, and low-Earth orbit is a long way from filling up. SpaceX and most other Western operators de-orbit their rockets and satellites at the end of their missions to prevent additional debris in orbit. “That said, the law of large numbers says that if it can happen, it will happen,” Nicolls said. There’s still a long way to go to improve tracking, awareness, and coordination in orbit. This is sometimes called space traffic management. No global treaties or regulations require satellites to broadcast their locations and maneuvers or mandate coordination among satellite operators. Without rules of the road, it is up to governments and companies to set norms of behavior. “Once you can track this debris, and if you can track it well, these are like obstacles on a road. If you can measure them, you can predict where they’re going to be, and you can avoid them,” Nicolls said. “What you can’t avoid is other operators who maneuver without telling you where they’re going to go. We have many examples of this on Starlink, unfortunately.” Nicolls presented several examples of satellites from other operators closely approaching Starlink satellites as they zip around the Earth at 5 miles per second. The examples did not suggest nefarious intent, such as spying or an attempt at striking a Starlink in orbit. Instead, the other satellites conducted maneuvers and changed their trajectories without their owners informing anyone. “Either they were not publishing their ephemeris to us, or they were not considering the ephemeris of others when they were maneuvering, and these led to conjunctions of tens of meters to hundreds of meters,” Nicolls said. “Way too close to comfort.” An ephemeris contains information about a satellite’s location and movement. Some operators may not want to share this information, believing secrecy might provide a competitive or strategic advantage. But if spaceflight is to be sustainable, it is vital to err on the side of openness rather than secrecy. “The minimum standard in low-Earth orbit needs to be that you know where your satellite is, and you know where it’s going. You need to be able to predict where your trajectory is, and that prediction needs to be reliable,” Nicolls said. SpaceX lauds itself as the benchmark for orbital stewardship. It pretty much has to be, with so many Starlinks in orbit and so much of its business relying on space. The company has lowered the altitude of the entire active Starlink constellation below 500 kilometers (310 miles), an adjustment that provides three benefits. It moved the network away from other heavily trafficked regions in low-Earth orbit, expedited reentry for any failed Starlink satellites, and improved service for Starlink customers. All of SpaceX’s new-generation Starlink V3 satellites, which began launching on the company’s Starship rocket last month, will fly even lower, at about 350 kilometers (217 miles). SpaceX also publishes ephemeris data for its Starlink satellites and has asked other operators to share their data, too. Without ephemeris sharing, operators rely on tracking data or their own satellites’ collision avoidance capabilities. Starlink satellites, for example, use onboard navigation cameras to detect other objects in orbit. SpaceX calls the system Stargaze, and it now tracks about 90 percent of maneuvering satellites that intersect Starlink orbits, Nicolls said. That is good, but it would be better if all operators agreed to be more open about what their satellites are doing. It is now a “fairly common occurrence” that Starlink satellites detect an upcoming close encounter and autonomously maneuver out of the way, Nicolls said. If there’s not enough to steer away from a close encounter, Starlink satellites can hunker down at the time of greatest danger. These “ducking maneuvers” involve pointing the satellite to minimize the chance of a collision, Nicolls said. Starlink satellites perform about 1,000 collision avoidance maneuvers per day to get out of the way of functioning satellites or bits of space debris. In the last few months, Nicolls said Starlink satellites have steered clear of about 650 active satellites from other operators. Only half of those 650 come from operators that participate in ephemeris sharing with SpaceX, he said. “In particular, it’s been very challenging to share data with international operators,” Nicolls said. “There has been good progress here. There have been some good stories here on how we’ve begun to share data. For example, we share data closely with the Chinese space station as well as some other Chinese satellite operators, but there are many that we do not, and we have no information about where those satellites are or where they’re going.” A chart in Nicolls’ presentation suggested the problem largely lies with Chinese and Russian satellites. But some in the United States, Europe, and Japan also decline to share. “My fundamental ask here is that we need to make headway here and begin sharing data across all vehicles, from whatever country you are, with us and with others, to promote a safe operating environment,” Nicolls said. What do SpaceX’s competitors think? Ars asked the head of Amazon Leo, Rajeev Badyal, about coordinating with Starlink, his chief competitor in the satellite broadband market. Amazon has 396 satellites in orbit today, with hundreds more awaiting launch when their rockets are ready to take them to orbit. “Space safety is paramount to us as well,” Badyal said. “Every launch we do, we actually coordinate with SpaceX. We coordinate with others who have satellites. We let them know where we’re going to drop them off, how we’re going to orbit raise, etc. So we have regular meetings with companies like SpaceX to exchange data. “So, for us, sharing ephemeris or sharing the information necessary for space safety is a non-issue,” Badyal said. “We’re big proponents of making sure that all of us operate in this domain in space in a coordinated fashion because [the] consequences of not doing so are significant for everybody, and there’s no reason not to.” Amazon Leo’s first-generation constellation will consist of 3,232 satellites, and the Federal Communications Commission in February approved Amazon’s request for a 4,504-satellite second-generation network. Amazon has filed for FCC approval for an additional 5,105 satellites for direct-to-device connectivity, but the company is still far from matching the size of the Starlink fleet. “Obviously, SpaceX is the largest constellation out there,” Badyal said. “We work with them hand in hand. In terms of China and adversaries, that’s something I can’t comment on at the moment. At some point, we will have to coordinate, or it’ll end up being a negative thing for everybody, especially if they get to scale.”

Judge weighs if Trump can charge $100K for early access to Truth Social posts
2026-10-08

Judge weighs if Trump can charge $100K for early access to Truth Social posts

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Likely in the next few weeks, a federal judge will decide if Donald Trump can become the first president to personally profit from selling early access to government information in his social media posts. At a hearing Wednesday, Department of Justice civil attorney Brantley Mayers argued that there was no conflict whatsoever with Trump charging up to $100,000 monthly for instant API access to Truth Social posts that often break news from his administration. Trump is the largest stakeholder and majority owner of Trump Media & Technology Group, the parent company that owns Truth Social, and the value of the API access comes largely from Trump’s role as president. News groups suing have argued that Trump’s exorbitant API fees violate the First Amendment and the Presidential Records Act by creating tiers of access to government information in social media posts that Trump doesn’t technically own. Additionally, Trump’s exorbitant API fees undermine the Fifth Amendment. That prohibits the government from “charging unreasonable sums that cannot be justified to offset the cost of the government benefit, and granting preferential access to crucial government information for arbitrary and irrational reasons,” news groups argued. However, Mayers likened Trump’s Truth Social posts—which have announced military strikes, ceasefires, tariffs, immigration policies, and major changes in his administration—to Franklin D. Roosevelt’s evening radio addresses known as “fireside chats,” Courthouse News reported. His personal feed acts as an unvarnished public service, not a greedy profit grab, the DOJ argued. It’s clear, though, that Trump could potentially make $1.2 million per subscriber annually by selling government information that is not Trump’s property. Before Mayers could proceed, US District Judge Paul Oetken “cut him off,” Courthouse News reported. “Well, President Roosevelt didn’t charge money for his fireside chats, did he?” Oetken asked. Responding, Mayers suggested that although Roosevelt didn’t charge for his broadcasts, there were similar “barriers to entry” keeping every American from tuning in. Just as some news groups today can’t afford to pay $60,000 to $100,000 for Truth’s API, some Americans in the ’30s and ’40s couldn’t afford radios, he argued. Nikhel Sus, chief counsel for a group representing news plaintiffs called the Citizens for Responsibility and Ethics in Washington (CREW), told Ars that the judge was right to push back on the DOJ’s comparison. During the hearing, the judge acknowledged that the Truth API could be discriminatory by creating “two classes of people,” Courthouse News reported. Sus said that he also clearly recognized the “symbiotic relationship” between Trump and Truth Social. Criticizing the comparison to FDR’s fireside chats, Sus told Ars that the DOJ “can throw out these hypotheticals that have absolutely nothing to do with this situation until the cows come home, but they’re not persuasive.” At the hearing, Oetken asked “tough questions” of both sides, Sus told Ars. No elected official has ever owned a media company where they can profit from presidential announcements, and the judge took time to remark that “there hasn’t really been anything like this case.” Talking to Ars, Sus argued that the lack of precedent should trigger alarm bells, not excuse the conduct, as the DOJ has suggested. “Just because no prior president had the gall to try to do something so flagrantly corrupt and illegal doesn’t mean that it is legal,” he said. Initially, Oetken approached claims that the API scheme violated the First Amendment with skepticism, Courthouse News reported. At one point, he wondered if Trump delaying the general public’s access to information by potentially as little as a fraction of a second would meaningfully harm the free press. Although it’s easy to see how an algorithmic trader would benefit from getting information just a little bit sooner, “It’s a negligible delay to a human being,” Oetken suggested. But the conflict isn’t only about timing, nonprofit news organizations have argued. TMTG has confirmed that large news groups are already in talks to buy API access because there is immense value in the service that goes beyond getting instant access to Truth Social posts. For example, the API “provides a machine-readable feed and a historic archive of all of President Trump’s Truth Social posts,” Sus told Ars. With that access, even if Trump deletes posts—which he frequently does—any news group can maintain a more reliable log of all posts. “It is absolutely worth something,” Sus told Ars. “And it’s not just the algorithmic traders. News outlets use APIs too.” The judge sees the case as complex and did not rule from the bench, instead promising a written ruling on whether to enjoin Trump from selling API access while he’s in office at a later date. Sus told Ars that courts typically treat requests for preliminary injunctions urgently and that he expects a decision in the next few weeks. Until then, news groups remain optimistic that the court’s ruling will swing their way, Sus said. “All we’re asking for is that when the president chooses to make government information available to the public, he has to do so on equal terms,” Sus said. “He cannot discriminate based on who’s willing to pay the president’s personal company hundreds of thousands of dollars. That is obscenely corrupt and unconstitutional.” Defending Trump’s scheme, Mayers asked the judge to look at Trump’s interests as wholly separate from the media group that bears his name. He claimed Trump had nothing to do with the decision to charge for API access to his posts, branding them as a “completely private commercial decision” made exclusively by TMTG. Nonprofit news groups suing, however, have argued that the expensive API pricing is “corrupt,” ignoring America’s long history of providing equal access to presidential records. On Wednesday, David Schulz, an attorney representing The Intercept, argued that the scheme gives a “direct advantage” to newsrooms that can pay the high price for previously free access. “Trump Media specifically is courting the largest news organizations in the country to get them to subscribe to Truth API,” Sus told Ars. If Trump’s gamble pays off and the judge approves the API sales, Sus warned that the press would suffer, as more politicians could follow his lead, and access to even more government information would be delayed behind paywalls. “If the president is allowed to do this, it sets a very dangerous precedent for enabling public officials to profit off of their office and specifically profit off of government information,” Sus told Ars.

Trump Mobile hack and apparent lack of FCC authorization raise security alarms
2026-10-08

Trump Mobile hack and apparent lack of FCC authorization raise security alarms

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Trump Mobile apparently failed to obtain necessary authorizations to operate the international calling portion of its phone service, Sen. Maggie Hassan (D-N.H.) wrote in a letter to the company today. Her letter said Trump Mobile also doesn’t appear to have submitted a required plan for fighting robocalls. Trump Mobile’s key business partner, Liberty Mobile Wireless, did make a robocall database filing, but it was not complete, Hassan said. Hassan’s letter raised questions about Trump Mobile’s security related to a report that hackers stole personal data of 3,615 customers and Trump Mobile’s previous confirmation that a vendor it uses exposed customers’ personal data on the Internet. Hassan alleged that Trump Mobile failed to follow multiple FCC filing rules that are supposed to help ensure the security of mobile services. “The apparent absence of authorization for Trump Mobile international services raises questions about who is providing or reselling the international telecommunications services advertised by Trump Mobile, the authority under which those services are being provided, and the company’s commitment to complying with requirements designed to safeguard national security,” Hassan told Trump Mobile CEO Patrick O’Brien. Referring to the recent data breach, Hassan said it’s worrisome that a ransomware group “claimed that when it notified Trump Mobile of the breach, the company responded that ‘[w]e have no team to handle this.’” Trump Mobile also seems to lack a robust customer authentication process and offers “streamlined service activation that can make it easier for scammers to obtain US numbers,” Hassan wrote. Trump Mobile, which has a trademark and name licensing deal with the Trump family, advertises that its customers can call more than 230 countries and territories. “Yet a search of the FCC’s International Communications Filing System suggests that Trump Mobile does not hold the necessary authorization for these services under Section 214 of the Communications Act of 1934,” wrote Hassan, the top Democrat on the US Congress Joint Economic Committee. Hassan said the authorization process requires disclosure of foreign ownership so the FCC can assess potential national security risks. These FCC filings are required both for carriers that operate their own networks and Mobile Virtual Network Operators (MVNOs) like Trump Mobile, which resell other carriers’ mobile service, Hassan’s letter said. Hassan’s letter cited a 2012 ruling against a different carrier, in which the FCC said “international telecommunications carriers that fail to obtain Section 214 authority may endanger important public interest considerations involving national security, law enforcement, foreign policy and trade policy.” She also cited the 2021 revocation of China Telecom Americas’ authorization, in which the FCC said that a carrier controlled by a foreign government could be used to “access, monitor, store, disrupt and/or misroute US communications” for the purpose of espionage. Hassan asked questions about Trump Mobile’s ties to Liberty Mobile Wireless, an MVNO based in Florida. Trump Mobile officials told The Verge in February that Liberty Mobile is the “enabler” and “backbone” of Trump Mobile, and that Liberty Mobile handles much of Trump Mobile’s technical, legal, and financial operations. Trump Mobile officials’ statements to The Verge indicated that Trump Mobile and Liberty Mobile are run by the same people. Hassan’s letter asked whether Trump Mobile owns, operates, or controls Liberty Mobile. Trump Mobile emerged last year, claiming it would sell a phone made entirely in the US. Trump Mobile quickly abandoned that claim but couldn’t deliver the promised phone on time. Preorders shipped nearly a year late, and the phone’s price increased from $499 to $749. Hassan’s letter noted that the phone itself is reportedly “a near exact match for the HTC U24 Pro, a phone launched in 2024 and manufactured in China.” Hassan said she found evidence that Trump Mobile and Liberty Mobile both failed to submit required information about efforts to verify customer identities. Hassan wrote: Concerningly, Trump Mobile and Liberty Mobile also seem to have weak scam prevention efforts. They do not appear to have included “a description of their ‘know-your-customer’ [KYC] processes in their robocall mitigation plan,” which is required in annual Robocall Mitigation Database (RMD) filings. Liberty Mobile’s most recent filing does not appear to include KYC information, and a search for Trump Mobile and T1 Mobile LLC, its operational business name, in the database yielded no filings under those names. Failure to comply with RMD rules can be punished with fines, removal from the database, and an order requiring other phone companies to block the offending carrier’s phone traffic. Hassan quoted an FCC enforcement advisory issued in February of this year, which she said “made it clear that compliance by MVNOs like Trump Mobile and Liberty Mobile is especially important because ‘[r]esellers of voice service that serve end-users are often in the best position to identify customers that are the source of apparently illegal traffic.’” Hassan’s letter asked O’Brien a series of questions related to the data breach, Trump Mobile’s ownership structure, its relationship with Liberty Mobile and other carriers, its systems for subscriber verification, compliance with various legal obligations, its FCC filings, device sales, and which countries supply parts for Trump phones. She requested a response by October 29. We contacted Trump Mobile, Liberty Mobile, and the FCC today and will update this article if we receive any comments. FCC Chairman Brendan Carr has proposed stricter Know-Your-Customer rules, but that doesn’t necessarily mean he would be quick to investigate Trump Mobile. Carr is an enthusiastic Trump supporter and backs Trump’s claim of power over historically independent agencies like the FCC. Carr recently refused calls to take action on a complaint that Trump commercials violate laws against using taxpayer funds for propaganda.
Fortune | FORTUNE
Disney offered a 13-year-old $2 million to sing in ‘The Lion King.’ His mom took $100K plus royalties instead. He’s still getting paid 34 years later
2026-10-09

Disney offered a 13-year-old $2 million to sing in ‘The Lion King.’ His mom took $100K plus royalties instead. He’s still getting paid 34 years later

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Becoming an overnight millionaire is every teenager’s dream. But when Disney offered 13-year-old Jason Weaver $2 million to sing as young Simba in The Lion King—his mom told them no. “When they offered that deal, it was really just to take this money and go,” Weaver once told VladTV in a 2019 interview that’s currently going viral on social media. “I think they knew and forecasted that they were gonna make a s–t ton of money.” It was a huge offer for a kid from Chicago. Weaver says his biggest paycheck until then was about $80,000, for playing a young Michael Jackson. “That amount of money to an average middle-class family in Chicago in the early ’90s … That’s something,” he said. But his mom and manager, Marilyn “Kitty” Haywood, wasn’t dazzled. Instead, she negotiated for royalties and about $100,000 upfront. And three decades on, the now 47-year-old “I Just Can’t Wait to Be King” singer is still getting paid—and has since made more than the $2 million he rejected. “If she hadn’t done that, that would have been one of the biggest mistakes of my career,” he added. As the saying goes, good things come to those who wait. Weaver’s mom said an upfront check could be the last he ever saw from the film. She’d noticed something: Disney kept rereleasing its old classics, decade after decade—a habit that began with the 1944 reissue of Snow White and the Seven Dwarfs. She’d also taken stock that Disney was on a roll: The casting call came hot off the heels of the success of Aladdin and Beauty and the Beast. “She was able to see the playing field and go, ‘Wait a minute, this is gonna make a lot of money over time, so what happens when my son turns 40? Is he still gonna be able to get a check from this when they eventually release this?’” Weaver recalled. “And sure enough, she was absolutely right.” It was a risky ask at the time. Weaver said that Disney wasn’t doing royalty deals for newcomers back then, only for “established huge pop stars.” But Haywood wasn’t new to the entertainment business. She sang with the Chicago group Kitty & the Haywoods, and backed Aretha Franklin—and she knew that the first offer is rarely the last. If Disney was willing to put $2 million on the table straight away, the film was clearly worth far more. She was right. The film has since been turned into a theme park, dolls, T-shirts, video games, and more. Next to it, Haywood said, $2 million looks small. “[It’s] hundreds of [millions], if not billions of dollars that they made off of this franchise,” as Weaver put it. Taking the cash upfront would have meant missing out on all of it. Plus, her gamble didn’t just beat Disney’s first offer. It gave Weaver—who admits that as a teenager, he probably would have blown the millions—an income that’s still paying out. Hand a 13-year-old $2 million, and it could be gone before he’s even old enough to drive. “I wasn’t thinking about a portfolio or diversifying,” Weaver said. After lending his vocal cords to Disney, Weaver went on to star in two sitcoms, Thea (1993–94) and Smart Guy (1997–99), where he played Marcus Henderson. He also landed film roles in Drumline, The Ladykillers, and ATL, and more recently roles in The Chi and Sistas. He kept singing, too. His debut album, Love Ambition, came out on Motown in 1995, and in 2004 he was featured on Chingy’s “One Call Away,” which peaked at No. 2 on the Billboard Hot 100. Weaver hasn’t disclosed his net worth or how much The Lion King still pays him. But he says the royalties keep coming, whether or not he’s got a job on. The Lion King has since grossed nearly $1 billion at the box office, and its 2019 remake pulled in more than $1.6 billion. Meanwhile, estimates put Weaver’s worth between $4 million and $7 million. Even at the lower figure, that’s about double the $2 million his mom turned down. Not only has Weaver, by his own account, made more than what Disney originally offered, but he continues to be shocked by how much it pays years on. “When I got my first royalty check, me and my mom thought it was a mistake,” he said. And 34 years later, the checks keep coming—and they won’t stop with him, either: Weaver agreed the royalties could be passed down to his kids, and their kids after that. “Honestly that income, that residual income that I generate—man that is so helpful,” he added. “Even nowadays, when I get a check, it’s like … yes!” Orianna Rosa Royle is the Success associate editor at Fortune, overseeing careers, leadership, and company culture coverage. She was previously the senior reporter at Management Today, Britain's longest-running publication for CEOs. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Elon Musk just lost his trillionaire status again after three days—but he says ‘it’s not like it’s sitting in a bank account’ anyway
2026-10-09

Elon Musk just lost his trillionaire status again after three days—but he says ‘it’s not like it’s sitting in a bank account’ anyway

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
In the span of just three days, SpaceX founder Elon Musk ascended to trillionaire status before crashing out of the club in a billion-dollar downturn. And while he remains the richest person in the world by a $600-billion longshot, the entrepreneur says his wealth is always at the whim of his companies’ success. “Trillionaire represents some percentage ownership in companies that I built, and it’s not sitting in a bank account,” Musk said in an interview with entrepreneur Peter Diamandis earlier this year, about three months before he first became a trillionaire following  SpaceX’s IPO in June. Musk’s net worth started to fall from its recent high of $1.05 trillion on Tuesday, dropping down to $987 billion on Thursday, according to the Bloomberg Billionaire Index. His aerospace company’s shares were down 3.5% and trading under $162 yesterday, just as his EV car business Tesla was also down over 2%. Forbes estimates that Musk lost $21 billion Wednesday morning when SpaceX shares fell following a report that the company plans to raise $40 billion to buy Nvidia chips. The strategy could have impacted shares due to potential concerns about the company’s spending needs and debt load, even as it invests heavily in AI infrastructure. It’s a steep $63 billion loss after he regained his trillionaire status this Monday; SpaceX shares rose nearly 8% at the start of the week, as Morgan Stanley analysts even called the stock “cheap” at the day’s prices. And as Musk owns around 4.76 billion shares of SpaceX—including another 1.3 billion shares of unvested restricted stock—his net worth shot up from $976.9 billion to $1.04 trillion afterward. Very few people—if any others at all—will ever reach a 13-figure net worth in their lifetimes. But the jaw-dropping number isn’t actually what it seems, Musk has said. And in 10 years, he’s predicted his fortune may mean nothing at all. “I own a percentage of the companies. The companies are doing lots of useful things, the value of the company grows,” the tech entrepreneur continued. “I own a percentage of the companies, and that sums up to that number, which seems high.” Musk is just one of the world’s richest entrepreneurs who have watched their net worths dramatically rise and fall with the motions of the stock market. Oracle founder Larry Ellison dethroned Musk as the richest person in the world briefly last year after his company’s breakout earnings report sent shares soaring by 36%. The now-82-year-old entrepreneur, who owns 40% of Oracle, experienced an eye-watering $101 billion wealth surge. But Ellison’s net worth fell by an estimated $34 billion just two days following Oracle’s stock surge, while Musk enjoyed a $35 billion gain, putting him back on top. And on the flipside, Michael Dell, who owns 40% of his namesake tech company, gained around $122 billion between September of this year and the same time in 2025—nearly doubling his wealth within the span of that year. Larry Page’s net worth had grown $16.3 billion in the same period, while Jeff Bezos watched his wealth surge $24.5 billion. But despite the massive wealth surge, Ray Dalio, the billionaire founder of Bridgewater Associates, has also echoed Musk in arguing that paper wealth and spendable money are two different things. Rising asset values can make people wealthier on paper, but they must sell those assets to turn that wealth into money they can actually spend. “Wealth is not the same as money,” Dalio said on Steven Bartlett’s The Diary of a CEO podcast earlier this year. “You see a lot of people getting wealthy but you can’t spend the wealth. You have to sell the wealth to get money because you can only spend money.” As billionaires’ fortunes balloon while many workers struggle to keep up with their paychecks, Musk, the richest person on earth—and occasionally, the only trillionaire—believes that monetary wealth will be less meaningful thanks to gains from AI. In response to how companies would earn returns in the era of AI, Musk told The Economist that “Money won’t matter in 2036.” The reason, he said, is that AI would take over so much human work that jobs will be voluntary, and cash itself would become irrelevant. Back in 2024, Musk also proposed the idea of “universal high income” to distribute money in a non-working world. The 55-year-old entrepreneur took inspiration from Iain M. Banks’ Culture sci-fi novels, which feature a world filled with intelligent robotic beings and no traditional jobs. “In those books, money doesn’t exist. It’s kind of interesting,” Musk said in an episode of the Moonshots with Peter Diamandis podcast earlier this year. “And my guess is, if you go out long enough—assuming there’s a continued improvement in AI and robotics, which seems likely—money will stop being relevant.” Emma Burleigh is a reporter at Fortune, covering success, careers, entrepreneurship, and personal finance. Jeff Bezos says a 3-day workweek and more single-income households are on the way thanks to AI: ‘It’s going to be difficult to hire people’
2026-10-09

Jeff Bezos says a 3-day workweek and more single-income households are on the way thanks to AI: ‘It’s going to be difficult to hire people’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Say goodbye to working Fridays—or possibly ever working at all. That’s the future that Amazon founder Jeff Bezos predicts AI could bring. In contrast to his tech peers like Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei and OpenAI leader Sam Altman, who have warned about the technology’s potential risks, Bezos recently revealed that he sees a not-so-distant world in which AI-driven productivity makes it easier for people to support themselves, in turn requiring fewer working hours. “Some people will decide, I can support my family working three days a week. That’s what productivity in the economy means,” Bezos told Fox News this week. He added that the abundance of wealth and resources in the AI era will even likely cause a labor shortage because many families will revert to single-income households, meaning one member won’t have to work if they don’t want to. “You’re not going to need to have a two-earner household. If you want it, fine. If you don’t want to have it, one of the people might drop out,” Bezos said. “…It’s going to be difficult to hire people, because the productivity in the economy will make it so that [if] people don’t want to work two jobs, they won’t have to.” While Bezos is bullish on AI’s long-term potential, he also acknowledged that investors face a more uncertain path as the technology reshapes the economy. When asked whether the AI boom is a bubble, Bezos said that it was “hard to know.” The technology itself is real, he argued, even if the rush to capitalize on it means money is flowing to businesses that may not ultimately succeed. “What I do know is the technology is real. I think this is a good thing for society,” Bezos continued, but added that: “It’s tricky for investors. Every experiment gets funded—the good ideas and the bad ideas. In the moment, in the tussle of this, it’s hard for investors to know the difference between the good ideas and the bad ideas.” Amazon, the company Bezos founded in his garage back in 1995, is one of many major businesses betting heavily on AI. It plans to spend over $200 billion on capital expenditures this year, largely thanks to investments in data centers. At the same time, Amazon has cut about 30,000 corporate roles since late last year, underscoring the tension between AI’s promise of greater productivity and the uncertainty facing workers as companies restructure. Bezos also stands to benefit from the AI boom through Blue Origin, his space exploration company, which has ambitions to build data centers in space and eventually expand human activity to the Moon and beyond. The company’s latest funding round reportedly raised $10 billion, valuing the firm at $140 billion—helping Bezos’ net worth soar to $365 billion. Bezos isn’t alone in predicting that AI will boost productivity and give workers more flexibility. But business leaders are split on just how dramatically the technology could reshape the workweek—and whether it could eventually make working optional altogether. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has said that AI will “probably” lead to a 4-day workweek, while JPMorgan Chase boss Jamie Dimon predicted has predicted employees could eventually work just 3.5 days a week. Bill Gates has gone even further, floating the possibility of a two-day workweek. But perhaps no executive has made more sweeping predictions than Elon Musk. The SpaceX founder has predicted that AI and robotics could usher in an era of “universal high income,” making money less relevant and freeing people from the need to work. Late last year, Musk said: “In less than 20 years—but maybe even as little as 10 or 15 years—the advancements in AI and robotics will bring us to the point where working is optional.” Preston Fore is a reporter on Fortune's Success team. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Anthropic is hiring a political programs boss to oversee ‘presidential engagement’ ahead of the 2028 election
2026-10-08

Anthropic is hiring a political programs boss to oversee ‘presidential engagement’ ahead of the 2028 election

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Anthropic is setting up a “presidential engagement” program ahead of the 2028 elections, building an in-house team that will work directly with U.S. presidential candidates from both parties on AI issues; help Anthropic’s leadership set political strategy; and run the company’s political funding program. The effort—which is taking shape before the November midterm elections and an impending initial public offering that could value Anthropic at as much as $2 trillion—underscores the extent to which government policy, and support of the White House in particular, have become critical to the AI industry. Anthropic is hiring a political programs lead to spearhead the effort and build the team. This person will “build and run Anthropic’s presidential engagement program for the 2028 cycle,” according to the job description that the company posted recently. Said person will “map the prospective fields in both parties, offer AI policy education to candidates and their advisors on equal terms, prepare Anthropic’s executives for those conversations, and plan ahead for the platform, convention, and transition periods. “Decisions made over the next two years will shape the future enormously,” reads the job description. “We want every serious candidate and elected official, in both parties, to understand the technology and the case for governing it well.” Tech companies lobbying the government is established practice—and OpenAI is similarly hiring a U.S. congressional lead to advance its legislative priorities—but Anthropic’s plan to create a dedicated in-house program to engage with presidential candidates struck some experts as unusual. “Companies have relationships with presidential campaigns through their government affairs people. Corporate people can be involved on the fundraising side and on the policy side,” said Bruce F. Freed, president of the nonpartisan Center for Political Accountability. “But I’ve not seen such a formalized engagement through a presidential election engagement committee. My reading is this is an AI company effort aimed at shaping or staving off government regulation or oversight.” Anthropic did not immediately respond to Fortune’s request for comment. Anthropic’s relationship with the Trump administration has been particularly rocky. In March, the Department of Defense designated Anthropic as a “supply-chain risk” after the company clashed with the Pentagon over the ways its technology could be used by the military. Dario Amodei, CEO and cofounder of Anthropic, has called for slowing the pace of AI development in order to shore up safeguards and has been a vocal advocate for government regulation of AI. That view is not shared by President Trump, who has stridently rejected any regulation that could cause the U.S. to fall behind China in the AI race. Last week, Amodei attended a White House event with several other AI industry CEOs and signed an accord committing to self-regulation. “Consider this their start of developing relationships with whomever will be in the White House in 2029,” Katie Harbath, founder and CEO of Anchor Change, a tech policy advisory firm, said of Anthropic’s presidential engagement program. Sen. Alex Padilla (D-Calif.) said Anthropic’s search for a political programs lead is “a reminder that the companies building frontier AI models are also building political operations alongside their products.” While companies have a right to participate in the country’s democracy and to educate policymakers about how AI technology works, “education and influence are not the same thing, and the public deserves to know where one ends and the other begins,” Padilla told Fortune in a statement urging Anthropic to share more details about the program. “It’s important that the rules for AI be set by elected representatives accountable to voters, informed by industry and by independent experts, researchers, and affected communities, and not by whichever company has the best-resourced political program,” Padilla said. Anthropic is already politically active, and sees itself as “equal parts research lab, policy think tank, and technology company,” according to the job description, which stresses that its political participation is bipartisan. In contrast to the Center for Political Accountability’s Freed, Harbath, the Anchor Change CEO, said that Anthropic’s political programs role did not strike her as unusual. “It sounds a lot like what my job at Facebook was and that a lot of other companies have,” said Harbath, who served as public policy director at Facebook for 10 years. “What’s unusual about it is that it includes running the political action committee money. Usually that was a separate thing.” In addition to candidate education and grassroots organizing, the role calls for running “the day-to-day of Anthropic’s political giving,” and helping employees who “choose to participate in politics do so in an informed way.” In April, Anthropic set up its first employee-funded political action committee (PAC), called AnthroPAC, which allows employees to contribute up to $5,000 per year, a legal cap. Other tech companies also have PACs, such as MetaPAC and AmazonPAC. Individuals at tech companies can donate much more outside of their capped PAC contributions, and with Anthropic’s upcoming IPO, its employees will likely have even deeper pockets for political spending. In July Amodei donated $1 million to a pro-AI-regulation super PAC called Public First, Politico reports. Other employees have also given hundreds of thousands of dollars to Public First and other political advocacy groups. OpenAI does not have any employee-funded PACs, a company policy it highlighted in a June 2026 blog post. Instead, OpenAI leadership has made personal political donations and attempts to curry favor with the Trump administration. CEO Sam Altman donated $1 million to Trump’s inauguration fund in 2024. In early 2025, he posted on X that Trump “will be incredible for the country in many ways!” OpenAI president Greg Brockman donated $25 million to the pro-AI super PAC Leading the Future, although he canceled his next planned $25 million donation after employee backlash, including employees setting up a rival super PAC to advocate for stricter AI safety laws. The broader context outside of Anthropic’s new role is also relevant, with AI being an increasingly hot political topic on both sides of the aisle. “AI is already a big issue in 2026, and I think it will become even more prominent in 2028,” said Sarah E. Kreps, a law and government professor and director of the Tech Policy Institute at Cornell University. She expects the conversation to move from “the abstract” to real-world concerns such as “jobs, data centers, and privacy.” While Anthropic is likely looking to improve relations with the current and future administration, Harbath noted that it may be difficult for Anthropic to earn trust with both political parties. “Most companies hire people with Democratic and Republican backgrounds for a reason—the campaigns trust people who come from their party and understand how they work.” Anthropic’s political programs lead role calls for someone with 12 or more years of experience working for campaigns, party committees, or political organizations, with senior-level experience in national politics and “a record of working credibly with both parties.” Still, Harbath expects the role, which lists a salary range of between $295,000 and $345,000, will be challenging for whoever takes it and high pressure. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Fortune | FORTUNE
Gwynne Shotwell’s $300 million SpaceX pledge to Trump Accounts shows billionaires a tax-smart way to give away their own company’s stock
2026-10-09

Gwynne Shotwell’s $300 million SpaceX pledge to Trump Accounts shows billionaires a tax-smart way to give away their own company’s stock

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
In a new type of philanthropy, billionaire SpaceX president Gwynne Shotwell has pledged to give more than 2 million American kids a piece of her company through Trump Accounts. Under new Treasury rules, the ultrawealthy can now do the same with theirs, and the tax code makes it an especially efficient way to give. The rules, which took effect Sept. 30, let wealthy donors, working through charities such as private foundations, put shares of publicly traded U.S. companies directly into Trump Accounts, the tax-advantaged investment accounts for children that launched July 4. As of Oct. 1, the Treasury had opened an account for nearly every eligible child under 18, or about 60 million kids. Previously, money in Trump Accounts went into low-cost index funds, which spread an investment across hundreds or thousands of companies, theoretically lowering the risk of losing everything if one company tanks. But the new rules make an exception for donated stock, opening the door for the ultrawealthy, whose fortunes are often tied up in shares of the companies they’ve built. “For a founder sitting on billions of dollars of appreciated stock, this could be an extraordinarily attractive way to give,” Kevin Gentry, founder and CEO of nonprofit fundraising consultancy TenX Strategies, told Fortune. Shotwell and her husband, Robert, said in July they would “gift a share of our SpaceX stock to a Trump Account for each of more than 2 million children across our great nation,” she wrote on X. The gift targets kids ages 11 to 17 in lower-income areas, with extra emphasis on those near the couple’s central Texas home. At the time, President Donald Trump valued the gift at $325 million, and as of Thursday (assuming the current share price) it would be worth about $330 million. Shotwell announced her pledge before Treasury finalized the rules, which now require stock gifts to come through a charity and win Treasury’s approval. Shotwell has been the only donor to announce a major stock gift, the Wall Street Journal  reported, but Treasury notes show others have expressed interest in similar donations. That raised the question of whether the world’s first trillionaire would participate. While Elon Musk has criticized other philanthropists like MacKenzie Scott and lamented the difficulties in giving effectively, he’s also hinted at giving his wealth away. SpaceX did not respond to Fortune’s request for comment. For the ultrawealthy who decide to practice philanthropy through Trump Accounts, the tax break looks a little different. “There is no tax deduction for giving either stock or money to Trump Accounts,” Scott Hanson, cofounder of wealth management firm Allworth Financial, told Fortune. Savings come later on. Only governments, tribal governments, and charities can make these stock gifts, so billionaires have to move their shares into a charity first. “The tax play is the stock they transfer to a foundation prior to gifting to a kid’s Trump Account,” Hanson said. Donors who give a private foundation publicly traded stock they’ve owned for more than a year can generally deduct its full market value, up to 20% of their adjusted gross income each year, with any excess carried forward for up to five years, according to the IRS. And because giving stock away isn’t a sale, “there is no taxable gain to tax,” Sherman Standberry, a CPA and CEO at tax consulting firm My CPA Coach, told Fortune. Gentry offered an example of why that matters. A founder who, say, paid $1 million for stock now worth $100 million would generally realize $99 million in gains by selling it, and likely owe significant taxes on that. He questioned: “Why sell an appreciated asset, pay the tax, and give what’s left when the tax code may allow you to give the asset itself?” While none of those perks are new, what is is where the stock can end up. Another potential upside for donors is a whole new class of shareholders, Gentry said. Treasury acknowledged that appeal in its rules, writing that donors hope a five-year holding period “may lead recipients and their families to feel that they have a stake in the fate of the corporation.” But that also ties some kids’ savings to a single company’s fortunes. The same rules acknowledge that kids “will bear some additional risk in the form of increased portfolio concentration” when donations come as stock rather than cash. Families, meanwhile, can’t choose the stock or, for now, refuse it. Shares generally must be held for five years, or until the end of the year a child turns 17. Gentry said a foundation handing out its founder’s stock raises legal questions, including over self-dealing, and its board should be ready to explain the decision. “The important question is whether the philanthropic purpose is driving the transaction,” he said. The Treasury declined Fortune‘s request for comment. Getting a job at Bending Spoons comes with no bonus, no fancy title, and a warning—800,000 people applied anyway
2026-10-09

Getting a job at Bending Spoons comes with no bonus, no fancy title, and a warning—800,000 people applied anyway

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Joining Bending Spoons, the Italian tech conglomerate that owns Vimeo and AOL, comes with a warning. Each prospective hire is sent a list of its “controversial” workplace principles, which advises candidates to prepare for “considerable” challenges, workloads, and expectations. Those that don’t fully commit, don’t get the job. Despite this, Bending Spoons is regularly inundated with job applications. Last year, it received 800,000 CVs but hired only 286 people—making its recruitment process 100-times more selective than the Ivy League. Chief executive Luca Ferrari once described Bending Spoons as “like private equity had a baby with Google.” It buys underperforming apps, rebuilds their technology with its own engineers and, unlike a typical buyout firm, keeps them. Since its founding in 2013, it has acquired more than 50 businesses. “People want to work here because they know that they’ll be taking on big challenges with talented colleagues every day,” says Nicolle Wasserman, head of people operations at the company. Bending Spoons ranks No. 69 on Fortune‘s 2026 list of the 100 Best Companies to Work For in Europe. The recruitment process is purposefully selective. About 60,000 applicants passed an initial CV screen and moved on to online tests designed to measure how quickly candidates solve unfamiliar problems and learn new skills. The tasks can take up to six hours, and some are monitored. Wasserman says traditional interviews tend to penalize candidates who are shy or are not native English speakers and to reward those who oversell themselves. “It’s easy for a candidate to overstate their skills or accomplishments in an interview, but it’s hard to misrepresent them in a practical test,” she says. To manage the volume of applicants, the company built its own recruiting software called Role Model. It draws on candidates’ test results and AI models trained on years of hiring data, including how past recruits went on to perform in the job. The company says this allows each member of its talent team to handle tens of thousands of applications a year. About 3,300 candidates reached interview stage, but it is a central talent team, not a hiring manager, that makes the final call. The company says this limits personal bias. Each recruit’s performance is tracked for up to two years and fed back into the company’s selection models. “We’ve gotten more selective in recent years, and much better at identifying predictors of success,” Wasserman adds. Even those that secure a job remain at risk. Bending Spoons says it parts ways with employees performing “adequately” if stronger contributors are available, something it acknowledges is uncommon. Wasserman describes the company’s employees, known internally as ‘Spooners’, as central to its success. The company employs 600 people and revenue per full-time employee has more than doubled in two years, from $1.12 million in 2023 to $2.57 million in 2025, according to its IPO filing. Bending Spoons flew out more than 500 of its employees from Italy to New York for its opening day on the Nasdaq in July—breaking the exchange’s record attendance figures. The IPO valued Bending Spoons at $18.4 billion and raised $1.68 billion, one of the largest by a European company this year. Despite the large amounts of revenue being brought in by its employees, Bending Spoons does not pay performance bonuses, which are common at other tech companies. It argues that pay tied to targets encourages short-term thinking without reliably improving results, and Wasserman says it makes relationships between colleagues “more transactional and less honest.” Instead, the company invests more heavily in salaries and reviews them annually. Employees can also purchase stock in the company directly through their salary at a discounted rate, and plans to continue offering this perk now it is listed. In 2025, 84% of eligible staff did so. Hierarchy is kept to a minimum. Internally, the company makes no distinction between junior and senior engineers, and managers are known simply as “leads.” In most cases, there are no more than three layers of management between the chief executive and a member of the core team. Externally, employees can describe themselves however they like on LinkedIn, “as long as it’s reasonable,” Wasserman says. She adds that the company spent so much time debating what separated one level from the next, and fielding requests for better titles, that it concluded the exercise was “an enormous waste of time and energy.” The company’s published principles are blunt about the demands of the job. It places responsibility for wellbeing on the individual. An employee bothered by Slack messages at night, for example, is expected to turn off notifications rather than look to the company for a policy, and someone who feels drained should adjust their schedule or take time off under a flexible vacation policy that requires no approval. This approach “chimes with our culture of freedom and responsibility,” Wasserman says. Publishing the principles allows candidates to judge whether they fit before committing to a demanding hiring process. “These things need to be addressed openly and in good faith so people can determine for themselves how aligned they are with the principles,” she says. “It really sucks for someone to go through a successful process, start working here, and swiftly realize they don’t see eye-to-eye on these topics.” For all Bending Spoons demands, unwanted departures are low. Wasserman says 0.6% of the core team quit in 2025—the rate so far this year has been even lower. Overall turnover was much higher, at 16.2%. This is partly due to Bending Spoons’ business model. The company made headcount reductions at OL, Eventbrite, and Vimeo, following the acquisitions. Once those businesses have been restructured later this year, it expects only a few hundred of the 1,830 full-time staff to remain, its SEC filing states. Having a leaner workforce comes with some benefits, according to Wasserman, allowing staff to take on more responsibility and work more flexibly. “A software engineer could spend a year rebuilding Evernote’s architecture, then six months rethinking subscriptions on Vimeo, then join a platform team building the payments technology every one of our businesses runs on,” she says. Nearly all of the company’s businesses and functions are led by people in their twenties or thirties, most of whom had little or no prior work experience. “It’s not unusual for someone still in their 20s to be leading a business doing hundreds of millions of dollars in revenue,” Wasserman says. The company acknowledges that its culture could be harder to sustain as it grows. In its filing, it warns that maintaining it may become more difficult across a larger, more dispersed organization with teams of different backgrounds and expectations. And with more than 1,000 potential acquisition targets identified, Bending Spoons is set to keep expanding. Sam Birchall is a features writer at Fortune 500 C-Suite Europe. Previously, she was a reporter at Raconteur, where she specialized in business and leadership storytelling for C-suite audiences. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved.
