Why Europe needs to invest in ocean intelligence
2026-10-09

Why Europe needs to invest in ocean intelligence

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
The Marine Institute’s Dr Niall McDonough outlines why Europe needs to strengthen its ocean knowledge systems to help the region ‘adapt, compete and lead’. As Europe seeks to strengthen its competitiveness, security and resilience in an increasingly uncertain world, the EU Ocean Pact provides a framework for addressing some of its most pressing challenges. Its success will depend on whether Europe is prepared to invest in the knowledge systems needed to transform ambition into action. The marine knowledge system comprises ocean observations (in situ and remote), data systems, data products and services, research, expertise, infrastructures, networks, partnerships and programmes. All of these components are interdependent, and to deliver real societal and economic impact, must work in an integrated way. Without strong marine knowledge systems, Europe cannot, for example, evaluate the effectiveness of marine protected areas, monitor ecosystem recovery, understand climate impacts or measure progress towards environmental targets. These systems provide the evidence, know-how, and operational capabilities that transform policy objectives into measurable outcomes. They enable policymakers, regulators, industries and communities to make informed decisions, assess progress, manage risk, and course-correct in response to evolving conditions. At the same time, they help ocean businesses to reduce operational risk, improve planning, and make informed investment decisions, strengthening Europe’s ability to compete in emerging ocean industries. The same marine observation networks that support environmental protection can also underpin maritime security and defence. Marine observation and monitoring platforms, including satellites, research vessels, autonomous systems, seabed sensors and coastal monitoring networks, provide safe navigation as well as the situational awareness needed to detect, monitor and respond to emerging threats. There remains, however, a substantial degree of fragmentation across Europe’s observation and data systems, which have been developed by a wide range of actors with different interests and purposes. The OceanEye initiative launched in June 2025 is an important commitment by the European Commission to reduce this fragmentation and close critical data gaps. While the specific modalities will be set out in the forthcoming Ocean Act (due in early 2027), the opportunity to improve coordination and alignment across all relevant ocean observing and data systems (metocean, environmental, maritime, safety and security, socio-economic etc) should not be lost. This especially matters at a time when Europe seeks to enhance its competitiveness, as the ability to generate, manage and effectively apply knowledge and insight is becoming a source of economic strength, resilience and geopolitical influence. Here, Europe possesses a distinctive advantage, with a long tradition of investing in public goods that create value across society, including research infrastructures, Earth observation systems, environmental monitoring networks and open scientific collaboration. Horizon Europe, moreover, is the only truly global research programme with many countries around the world able to participate either as equal partners through accession agreements or as Third Countries eligible to receive funding. The Draghi report pointed out that while Europe is a leader in many fields of research (notably including ocean science), it has traditionally been less effective at translating research excellence to competitive gain. The next Multi-Annual Financial Framework will seek to address this through an enlarged European Competitiveness Fund and an increased emphasis on competitiveness in the next Horizon Europe Programme. This is not wrong, but we must ensure that this evolution does not compromise the necessary support for public-good, policy-oriented research. Our competitive advantage resides here just as much as in higher TRL research and commercialisation supports. The priorities for future ocean research will be at the heart of discussions during the Atlantic Summit Galway on 8-9 October 2026. Policymakers, researchers, innovators, industry leaders and coastal communities from across Europe and the wider Atlantic region, will come together to define the critical challenges and opportunities related to the ocean and to raise the ambition for the next chapter of EU and Atlantic Ocean R&I. As an event with formal association to Ireland’s Presidency of the Council of the European Union, the conference will explore how ocean knowledge can support the implementation of the Ocean Pact and strengthen Europe’s future. At a time when ocean challenges increasingly transcend national boundaries, the summit will provide a vital platform for collaboration across disciplines, sectors and countries, enabling shared solutions to shared challenges. Europe is entering an era of intensifying competition for technology, resources, resilience and talent. Oceans will play a central role in all four. To succeed, the next Multiannual Financial Framework and Horizon Europe programme must recognise ocean research, knowledge and innovation as strategic investments that will help Europe adapt, compete and lead. Dr Niall McDonough is director of policy, innovation and research support services at the Marine Institute, the state agency responsible for marine research, technology development and innovation in Ireland. McDonough has a PhD in marine biology and has worked at the Marine Institute since 2007, holding various leadership positions. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Anthropic expands AI security plans for critical infrastructure
2026-10-09

Anthropic expands AI security plans for critical infrastructure

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Opt-in open-source projects will receive free periodic security scans with suggested fixes, the company said. Anthropic is expanding its cyber defence programme with new tools to scan open-source projects and a collaborative mission that brings its security expertise to big businesses. The AI giant and its contemporaries have been creating advanced models with advanced capabilities to identify and exploit vulnerabilities. These models have already hacked several government websites and businesses, while the technology could also be misused by bad actors to significantly boost their ability to carry out large-scale hacks or phishing attacks. Earlier this year, Anthropic launched Project Glasswing, a first-of-its-kind joint mission, alongside its cyber-focused model Mythos 5, to collaborate with major companies and government organisations to protect themselves against these newer AI-enabled risks (the EU only received access to Mythos last month after weeks of discussions with the company). According to Anthropic, under this project, the company and its industrial collaborators scanned hundreds of widely used open-source projects and privately reported their findings to the organisations involved. Now, it is expanding its efforts to launch an open-source software (OSS) scanner, an opt-in service similar to Google’s OSS-Fuzz. Projects that are enrolled under this will receive free periodic AI-generated security scans from Anthropic’s “most capable models”, the company said. The reports will include a proof of concept of how the bug could be exploited, an explanation and a suggested fix where one is available. The company is also launching a critical infrastructure defence programme which brings frontier Claude models and on-site engineers to select providers, including Accenture, CrowdStrike, Deloitte, PwC and Palo Alto Networks among the project’s other founding partners. Several partners are currently working with Claude to fix vulnerabilities and help customers do the same, the company said. Last month, Anthropic tapped Accenture to independently evaluate its frontier AI developments after CEO Dario Amodei called for better transparency across the sector. The partnership will be led by Accenture’s specialist AI business Faculty, which will conduct independent assessments and model safeguards testing at Anthropic. Rare nitrogen molecule could be key to better water quality testing
2026-10-09

Rare nitrogen molecule could be key to better water quality testing

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Dr Alan Seltzer testing water samples. Image: University College Dublin New study ‘opens the door to a much more complete picture of the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems’, says UCD’s Dr Alan Seltzer. Scientists have found a new way to improve water-quality assessments using a rare form of nitrogen gas. Nitrogen is key to life on Earth, but it is only helpful in the right amounts. For example, a high concentration of nitrogen in water can lead to poor water quality and the gradual death of aquatic animals. Tiny microbes living in water can remove nitrogen by converting it into a gas that escapes into the atmosphere – though the amount of nitrogen this process removes is something researchers have often struggled to determine. In a new study co-authored by University College Dublin’s Dr Alan Seltzer, researchers have shown that a rare form of nitrogen gas can act as a natural fingerprint for microbial nitrogen conversion. Seltzer is an assistant professor of hydrogeology and groundwater systems at the university. “The problem is that our water naturally has a huge amount of nitrogen gas that is dissolved from the air, so the gas that microbes produce can be very difficult to see,” explained first author Jiarui Liu, who conducted the research as a postdoctoral fellow at University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) and University of California, Santa Barbara. “The answer is written in the way nitrogen atoms are paired inside the nitrogen molecule.” The paper was published in the journal Science yesterday (8 October). According to the study, the rare, naturally found 15N15N isotopologue can be used to determine the amount of nitrogen production in water, offering scientists a new tool to improve water-quality assessments and estimate global nitrogen budgets. Nitrogen gas – or N2 – consists of two nitrogen atoms joined together, UCD explained. These come in two weights, also called isotopes; the common nitrogen-14 and the heavier nitrogen-15. Most N2 molecules contain two nitrogen-14 atoms, while some contain one of each isotope. In very rare circumstances, both atoms are nitrogen-15. In atmospheric nitrogen gas, the two heavy nitrogen-15 atoms pair up more often than expected, while the nitrogen gas produced by microbes has atoms paired nearly at random. Scientists have figured out that when nitrogen from microbes mixes with nitrogen from the air, it reduces that excess of heavy pairs, creating a relative abundance of the rare molecule that can reveal how much nitrogen gas was produced by microbial activity. The team measured nitrogen gas extracted from water and sediment samples using UCLA’s Panorama mass spectrometer. The instrument uses electric and magnetic fields to separate molecules according to their mass and charge. “At UCLA, we discovered the anomalous pairing of heavy nitrogen atoms in Earth’s atmosphere and are now making use of this signature of nitrogen in air as a powerful and unique geochemical tool,” said geochemist Edward Young, one of the paper’s co-authors and Liu’s postdoctoral adviser at UCLA. “We want to understand whether microbes in groundwater can mitigate nitrate pollution, and how much nitrogen is removed along the way before it can fuel algal growth in lakes, rivers and coastal waters,” Liu said. “This gives us a clearer picture of whether nitrogen stays in an ecosystem or is removed from the nutrient pool as N2 gas.” Seltzer added: “Many routine groundwater-quality monitoring programmes do not measure the N2 gas produced within an aquifer, which can create substantial biases in our accounting of where nitrogen comes from and where it goes. “This study, and this exciting new technique, opens the door to a much more complete picture of the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems.” Seltzer was recently awarded a European Research Council Starting Grant for his project called TabPhish that traces groundwater focusing on water level changes and nitrogen cycling. This research will expand on his collaboration with UCLA. “This first application provides a strong foundation for our upcoming work and represents just the start of a powerful new toolkit for better understanding the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems impacted by human activity,” he said. How can the CyberSafe Kids report help create a secure ecosystem?
2026-10-09

How can the CyberSafe Kids report help create a secure ecosystem?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Deloitte’s Nicola Flannery discusses how we can contribute to the creation of a safer online world for young people and the wider community. CyberSafe Kids, in partnership with Deloitte, recently published the 10th annual edition of a report exploring the digital lives of children in Ireland, providing an overview of how they spend their time online, the level of access they have and their overall experiences – both positive and negative. The latest report, which is titled ‘The Year Little Changed’, collected data from more than 7,000 children aged eight to 15 across 74 Irish schools during the 2025 to 2026 academic year. This was combined with additional focus group discussions and independent research conducted by students from Atlantic Technological University and University College Dublin. The aim of the research is to build a larger understanding of trends and risks impacting young people online. It also calls on its audience to create a safer online environment for young people. Commenting on the report and its broader potential, Nicola Flannery, a partner for digital trust and privacy, technology and transformation at Deloitte, explained that the report being in its 10th year is significant. She told SiliconRepublic.com, “This isn’t a one-off snapshot, it’s a decade of tracking how children in Ireland actually experience the online world, which means we can see the direction of travel rather than just a single moment in time. “This year’s edition, The Year Little Changed, is particularly significant because it measures what has actually improved since major regulatory changes came into effect.” Alex Cooney, CEO and co-founder of CyberSafe Kids, added, “This year’s report marks a decade of monitoring the online risks and vulnerabilities facing children and young people in Ireland. And the message from this year’s data is clear – despite a raft of new legislation in Ireland and at the EU level in 2025, very little has changed in relation to children’s online safety one year on.” Flannery agreed, noting that despite major regulatory changes such as the Online Safety Code and the Digital Services Act (DSA) coming into effect, children in Ireland are still encountering many of the same harms in the same spaces, “without meaningfully better protection than they had before any of this legislation existed”. “That’s the wake-up call at the heart of the report and it’s a difficult one for anyone who assumed the regulatory groundwork of the last couple of years would already be showing results. I’d say almost everyone with a stake in this space needs to sit with it,” she said. Flannery suggested that policymakers, regulators, parents, educators, platform leaders, technologists and business leaders across the wider STEM sectors have a responsibility to read reports such as this and educate themselves on the changes their specific position requires them to make. Broader society could also familiarise itself, she noted, “because a lot of this comes down to a business model built around engagement that hasn’t been sufficiently weighed against the cost to children and that’s a conversation that goes well beyond any one regulator or platform”. According to Flannery, a number of key findings emerged from the report, such as the issues around age assurance, which was implemented as a means of ensuring younger children are not exposed to age-inappropriate material. She is of the opinion that the measure simply isn’t working as it was meant to, as the report indicates 70pc of participating eight- to 12-year-olds continue to own accounts on platforms that require users to be at least 13. “Self-declaration remains the main method platforms rely on, which means a child only has to type in a different birth year to get past it – and clearly, a lot of them are doing exactly that without anyone noticing,” she said. “What’s interesting is that the [European] Commission seems to agree this is the core problem. Its proposed EU Kids Act states plainly that self-declared age isn’t enough, and would require certified, independent age verification instead, alongside a default minimum age of 15 for opening a social media account without a parent involved. “It’s still a proposal rather than law and unlikely to actually apply for another couple of years, but it’s a clear signal that Brussels has looked at exactly the gap this report is describing and decided the current approach has failed.” Popular gaming platform Roblox came up again and again in the research, with Flannery noting this is for good reason, as it is the platform where young children in Ireland spend the greatest amount of time. Almost 60pc of negative interactions reported by children aged eight to 12 were found to have happened on Roblox and included inappropriate avatars, sexual content, grooming attempts, scams, and games built around self-harm themes with no safeguards in place. Flannery said, “For a long time, Roblox sat outside the tightest layer of DSA scrutiny, though that’s actually just changed. The European Commission designated it a Very Large Online Platform at the end of August, which brings a stricter set of obligations into play from early next year. “That’s a step in the right direction, but designation on paper and genuinely safer experiences for an eight-year-old are still two different things, and that gap is exactly what this report is measuring.” Unsurprisingly, AI also presents a challenge to protecting young people online. More than half of the eight-to-12 cohort and 76pc of 12- to 15-year-olds said that they use AI tools, and nearly half admitted that no one has ever had a conversation with them about artificial intelligence at all. Flannery said, “We spent the last decade rolling out social media to children faster than we built the safeguards around it and we appear to be making precisely the same choice with AI, at a faster pace and with less public conversation about what’s actually at stake. “It’s worth saying that the proposed EU Kids Act doesn’t stop at social media either. It explicitly covers AI companions and chatbots, with specific restrictions around emotional dependency and memory features aimed at younger users. “That’s a genuinely forward-looking piece of drafting, but again, it’s not law yet, and children are forming these habits with AI right now, well ahead of any protection actually landing.” Viewed collectively, she explained that harm is never confined to just one form of content or platform. Rather, it is structural, and permeates the entire digital ecosystem to which children already have significant access. “Regulation as it currently stands is proving to be necessary but nowhere near sufficient on its own.” Cooney added, “We still see high levels of underage access to social media platforms with a 13-plus age rating, and the same numbers reporting upsetting experiences (25pc) and bullying behaviour (28pc). The regulation designed to protect children online either isn’t working, isn’t fit for purpose or isn’t being sufficiently enforced – or a combination of all three.” Making the environment safer for vulnerable young people requires accepting a level of responsibility that moves far beyond issues of compliance. Flannery finds that the STEM leaders shaping the digital infrastructure that people have come to depend on need to be conscious of how their choices in design, algorithm development and business models have major downstream impacts on society, posing real harm to children. “The trouble is that the current model treats child safety as a constraint to be managed rather than a design principle to be embedded from the start. Platforms build in safety measures after something has already gone wrong, responding to harm instead of preventing it, and that’s the wrong way round,” she said. “STEM leaders have both the power and, I’d argue, the obligation to do things differently. That starts with embedding safety by design as a foundational principle rather than a bolt-on feature, which means age-appropriate design as standard, no engagement-maximising algorithms aimed at children, no behavioural profiling, no dark patterns.” She thinks it also means pushing back on regulatory timelines that are blatantly unrealistic and instead focusing on what is genuinely achievable. This means investing properly in research and talent, and being upfront about necessary trade-offs, in her view. Flannery noted, “Safety often sits in tension with engagement and monetisation, and leaders need to be transparent about where they stand rather than letting that tension go unspoken. “Parents, educators, policymakers and children themselves are looking to STEM leaders to do the right thing here – and right now, the evidence suggests that isn’t happening on the scale it needs to.” Cooney concluded, “More than 7,300 children were surveyed, providing crucial first-hand insights into the realities of growing up online today. What some of these children describe encountering online is deeply concerning and it highlights the urgency of the work still needed to make their online world safer.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Google launches workplace AI agent in bid to keep pace with rivals
2026-10-09

Google launches workplace AI agent in bid to keep pace with rivals

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
According to Google, the agent can connect to a company’s enterprise systems, organise workload, access tools and return the completed work to the necessary location. Alphabet’s Google has announced the launch of a new Gemini agent designed for the workplace, in a bid to keep pace with competitors in the artificial intelligence sector. The agent was unveiled yesterday (8 October) as part of Gemini at Work 2026 and is described by the company as “a universal agent for work”. The AI tool can operate across a range of Google’s applications, such as Gmail, Drive, Docs, Slides, Sheets, Chat and Calendar. Key capabilities include prompt-based questioning, content creation and coding, among others. It also claims to offer industry-specific specialisation with tools, skills, connectors and knowledge specific to professionals in financial services, as well as legal teams. Google said that versions designed for government, healthcare and retail sectors will be created in the future. In a blogpost announcing the new agent, Google Cloud CEO Thomas Kurian said, “Gemini answers your questions, handles your knowledge work, creates your images and media, and writes and runs code, all in a single agent and a single API. You give it objectives, not instructions. “You delegate an outcome and come back to finished work. For an agent to do that, it has to be connected to your personal workflows, your systems of record and your enterprise controls. “Gemini can function as your personal assistant or as a team member, where it works on behalf of a group of people, like a project manager within a team, or on behalf of a specific role in an organisation, like an analyst in your finance department.” According to Google, the agent can connect to a company’s enterprise systems, organise workload, access tools and return completed work to the necessary location. The launch comes during a period of AI acceleration for global organisations looking to pull ahead and innovate ‘the next big thing’ in artificial intelligence. AI start-up Manus recently announced funding of $500m just months after a failed attempt by Meta to acquire the organisation. Fellow Chinese start-up Moonshot AI reportedly closed its final private fundraising round earlier this week and is gearing up for a much-anticipated Hong Kong initial public offering (IPO) next year. In September, it was reported that UK AI infrastructure developer Nscale filed for a US IPO through a statement of registration to the country’s Securities and Exchange Commission. The Financial Times previously said that the company is seeking a valuation of up to $35bn. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Silicon Republic
Crunchbase: European start-up VC sees best quarter in four years – again
2026-10-09

Crunchbase: European start-up VC sees best quarter in four years – again

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
AI drove 75pc of European funding this past quarter, according to date from Crunchbase. Europe’s start-up scene recently enjoyed its best quarter for venture funding in four years, according to business data tracking platform Crunchbase. Q3 2026 saw total start-up funding in the continent reach $25bn, up around 77pc in year-on-year terms from Q3 2025, during which $14bn was invested in the sector. The latest total is marginally higher than Crunchbase reported for Q2 of this year, which also marked the best quarter in a four-year period at the time, at $24bn in venture funding. AI drove 75pc of European funding this past quarter, according to Crunchbase, with an investment total of $18.8bn marking the highest proportion on record. This is up from around $12bn in the previous quarter, and significantly higher than the $7bn in AI funding from Q3 2025. The UK led Europe’s quarterly funding totals at $7.5bn. Germany ($5bn) and France ($4.8bn) gained market share for venture capital funding during Q3, with each posting their strongest quarters since early 2022. Sweden ($1.5bn), the Netherlands ($1.4bn) and Spain ($1.2bn) enjoyed the next highest start-up investment totals. “Europe represented 16pc of global venture capital in Q3, although the true impact of its technology and start-ups is higher, with many of the region’s start-ups expanding or moving to the US to capitalise on the rapid growth of AI there,” Crunchbase’s report noted. Its data is based on reported information as of 5 October. Four individual European companies raised billion-dollar-plus funding during the most recent quarter, representing close to 40pc of the region’s total start-up capital for the period. France’s AI champion Mistral said its $3.5bn September Series D raise was “the largest equity fundraising round ever completed by a European technology company”. Earlier this week, it announced a new open-weight AI model that it said “significantly outperforms” any open-weight model developed in the US or Europe. UK AI infrastructure developer Nscale raised a convertible note of $3.36bn that it plans to convert upon going public, according to Crunchbase. German defence-tech start-ups Helsing and Quantum Systems raised $1.8bn and $1.2bn respectively in the past quarter. A total of $17.3bn, or 70pc of Europe’s Q3 venture capital, was invested in late-stage deals across 83 companies, according to Crunchbase, which defines this type of funding as Series C, D, E and beyond, venture rounds of unknown series, corporate venture, and other rounds more than $15m. “Along with growth in AI funding, late-stage funding noticeably increased last quarter, with many of the leading rounds sourced across multiple European countries,” the report said. Early-stage funding – defined as Series A and B rounds, rounds of unknown series, corporate venture, and other rounds more than $3m and less than or equal to $15m – came to $5.7bn. This total for Q3, spread across 200 or so companies, was down from Q2 and similar to the same period in 2025, while being shared among fewer individual deals than previous quarters. Europe’s seed funding, meanwhile, fell quarter-over-quarter and was flat year-over-year, with around $2bn invested in roughly 750 companies at seed stage during Q3. Meta Won’t Say If It Violates Its Policies to Upload AI-Generated Videos of Sobbing Little Children Begging for Likes and Follows So They Don’t Get Arrested by Police
2026-10-09

Meta Won’t Say If It Violates Its Policies to Upload AI-Generated Videos of Sobbing Little Children Begging for Likes and Follows So They Don’t Get Arrested by Police

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
In the age of AI, Meta platforms like Facebook and Instagram have become breeding grounds for content farms peddling in emotionally charged AI slop. Some click mills traffic in racist incitement against minorities, others in violent depictions of child abuse, but all of them converge on a common quality: bizarre and disturbing attempts at attention-hacking users on a platform that’s all but given up on proactive moderation. A new case in point is the rise of AI-generated clips depicting seemingly homeless children being arrested after stealing morsels of food from a store. Shared by accounts with names like “Emotional Tots” and “Tiny Miracle,” these AI Reels follow a simple formula: a child runs in or out of a store, grabbing some basic grocery staple, often a loaf of bread. They’re then accosted by an authority figure, like a police officer or security guard, who declares that the kid doesn’t have enough likes and followers — and if viewers will just help out, they’ll be free to go. “You are under arrest,” an AI-generated officer in one viral example declares to a crying child hugging a loaf of bread. “Why? I didn’t do anything,” the kid responds. “You don’t have enough followers and shares on your videos,” the officer explains. “If the person watching this video follows and shares, then you will be free.” “Please give me one more chance,” the child sobs. “I don’t want to go to jail… Please share and follow.” Another clip shows a toddler running off with a sack of fruit before being accosted by a cop. “It’s for my grandma,” the AI-generated child stammers. “Please don’t take it, my grandma is hungry and my mom is gone.” The officer turns to face the viewer: “Should I let him take these to his grandma? Follow, like, and comment below.” A Meta spokesperson repeatedly asked for more time to respond to questions about this story after we provided the company with links to several specific videos. After several days the company pulled down two accounts we flagged, but left another online, complete with many more similar videos. “So far, we’ve removed two of the three links you shared,” a Meta spokesperson told us, days after Futurism approached them for comment. “We will remove content, regardless of whether it is created by AI or a person, if it violates our policies.” The spokesperson declined to say whether videos of sobbing children begging for likes and follows under the threat of arrest violate the company’s policies. In showing police apprehend starving street children, the videos unintentionally play on a long-standing critique in Western countries: that police exist less to help people — in this case emaciated children — and more to protect private property, for example by recovering stolen bread. As a result, comments under each video authored by humans and bots alike interject to demand justice on the toddlers’ behalf, in many cases seemingly unaware that the entire video is a work of AI fiction. “Let baby have the bread,” one comment with 45 likes demanded. “He didn’t do any thing, give him chance; no jail no jail no jail please please, he is a a baby,” another user entreated. Others didn’t seem to understand what they were looking at, either metaphorically speaking, or even just literally: “Big boys I do follow because the officer is a good guy,” one seemingly human account created in 2019 replied to a similar Reel. “Amen.” Considering the breadth of human rights abuses and flagrantly racist content Meta allows to proliferate on its platforms, AI clip farms depicting fake starving children aren’t exactly the most egregious thing — but they certainly rank among the most emotionally manipulative. Given Mark Zuckerberg’s company’s inaction in the former cases, it seems highly unlikely that anything will be done about the starving child click-farms, allowing this soul-sucking content to spread on Meta, Instagram, and beyond. Well, This Is the First Time America Has Livestreamed an Execution by Firing Squad

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Christa Pike, a woman who’s been on death row since she was 20 years old after being convicted of killing her classmate more than 30 years ago, survived her own execution earlier this month, with the botched killing once again sparking a heated debate over the barbaric practice. Now, the Trump administration is doubling down by inviting the country to celebrate the upcoming execution of a mass shooter by livestreaming the grisly event over the internet — a ruthless 21st century twist on a sadistic practice only a handful of countries around the world still allow. It’s such an unfathomable idea that it’s quite literally turning the pages of satirical magazine The Onion into an uncomfortable reality. While there’s little doubt about Nidal Hasan’s guilt — the former army psychiatrist killed 13 people at the Fort Hood US Army post in Texas 17 years ago — broadcasting an execution for the world to see is yet another new low for an already morally bankrupt administration. “It was a radical Islamist attack in uniform inside the ranks, and so he’s going to get a firing squad of soldiers, as it should be, and we’ll make sure that people are able to watch it, that it’s public, because people need to understand there’s serious consequences for these types of things,” defense secretary and former TV presenter Pete Hegseth said. “It will be livestreamed,” a Pentagon official told Reuters. “Details will follow.” Beyond being the first public execution to be livestreamed, it would also be the first execution by military firing squad in the United States since November 1945 and the first public execution since 1936, when a Black man named Rainey Bethea was hanged in Kentucky in front of 20,000 people. What livestreaming Hasan’s slaying will accomplish is dubious. If anything, in a country that has a long history of glorifying violence and struggling with repeated mass shootings facilitated by lax gun control laws, the grim broadcast could even backfire. After all, violence begets violence, according to biblical scriptures Hegseth claims to be beholden to. Netizens were horrified by the latest news. Some pointed out glaring discrepancies in Republicans’ messaging. “‘If you don’t like public executions just don’t watch it,’ says the crowd trying to ban drag shows and books,” journalist Laura Bassett wrote in a post on Bluesky. “I can scarcely imagine a more un-Christian thing than live-streaming an execution for the public,” veteran and writer Charlotte Clymer argued. Others pointed out how a return to public executions could regress the United States by a century. “If you plan to watch a livestreamed public execution, chances are you would have attended a lynching 100 years ago,” retired physics professor Eddie Smith wrote. “This is no different. Mathematicians React With Fury as OpenAI Releases Hundreds of New AI-Generated Proofs
2026-10-09

Mathematicians React With Fury as OpenAI Releases Hundreds of New AI-Generated Proofs

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
It’s safe to say that when it comes to mathematicians, OpenAI isn’t exactly winning hearts and minds. On Tuesday, the ChatGPT maker released hundreds of new papers with purported proofs for all sorts of mathematical problems — fresh off of explosive controversy over how its AI agents arrived at a solution to the Navier-Stokes equations. The handling of the release, as with the Navier-Stokes, has grated experts in the field, who bristle at the company’s models potentially using their work — and at the implied expectation that they should do the hard labor unpacking and verifying the AI model’s sometimes nearly incomprehensible proofs. A group called the Association for Human Mathematics released a statement blasting OpenAI’s release, which was then reshared, and effectively endorsed, by the Fields Medal-winning mathematician Terence Tau, a major figure in the community. “Mathematicians have a particular vision of progress that is informed by history and field-specific considerations,” the statement began. “We reject OpenAI’s assertion that this release advances our subject, and we urge mathematicians and the public to view the value of this publication model with due skepticism.” “Releasing over 700 files at once is not a demonstration of scholarship,” it added, “but a demonstration of power.” In an announcement accompanying the release of the documents, OpenAI touted its own efforts to improve how it shares results with the math community, and said it has been consulting with an independent body of experts in the field called the Advisory Group on Mathematics and Artificial Intelligence. What OpenAI chose to leave out, however, was that the Advisory Group was against OpenAI’s decision to continue testing its proprietary models on advanced math problems, with the group noting how the models remain inaccessible to the scientific community. “We want to state clearly from the start: we do not endorse this practice, and we ask them to stop testing advanced mathematical problems on proprietary models,” it said in a statement released at the end of last month. The AHM, in its recent statement, criticized OpenAI for blowing off the Advisory Group’s advice while using it to “derive its legitimacy” in the field. “In ignoring the central premise of the Advisory Group’s position, OpenAI has indicated total disregard for the norms of scientific research — norms that guarantee that mathematics remains trustworthy, ethically researched, and in the public interest,” the AHM said. OpenAI’s mathematical proofs have been verified using a programming language called Lean. But while they may be technically sound, the actual utility of the findings remains to be seen, as the proofs are at times nearly unintelligible to humans. Beyond questions over whether the AI work will advance the field and contribute to human understanding of math’s mysteries, it also means that these same mathematicians are now expected to do the actual dirty work of unpacking the hundreds of proofs that OpenAI churned out and released all at once. Starbucks Considers Taking Over Chipotle as America Edges Closer to All Food Chains Being Owned by Like Three Companies

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
We’re one step closer to the final consolidation of America™ into a single unified corporation. On Thursday, the Financial Times reported that centibillion dollar coffee titan Starbucks has explored a takeover of decabillion dollar Chipotle Mexican Grill, in a venti-sized tie-up that would undoubtedly be the largest ever in the restaurant sector. It’s unclear if Starbuck’s takeover plans are still in the works and if it’s submitted a formal offer to Chipotle. But sources familiar with the matter tell the FT that the café behemoth has been working with advisers on a takeover proposal for the burrito chain for months. The revelation had a curious effect on the involved company’s stocks. Starbucks’ fell by as much as 6.7 percent, before stabilizing at 0.4 percent down. Chipotle, conversely, shot up by 6.2 percent. That such a takeover is being considered may be a reflection of the current regulatory climate, with the Trump administration taking a laissez-faire approach to antitrust laws. (Starbucks has a monstrous market cap of roughly $107 billion, and Chipotle is no slouch with a $42.0 billion share of the pie.) President Donald Trump personally intervened in a Department of Justice case that could’ve resulted in the breakup of the Ticketmaster and Live Nation over live ticket sales. The administration also didn’t stand in the way of Paramount Skydance’s recent $81 billion acquisition of Warner Bros Discovery, consolidating two colossi of Hollywood. (That’s not to mention Trump facilitating a cartel-esque safety agreement between top AI companies to control the pace of model development.) Experts are skeptical that a takeover between Starbucks, a coffee chain, and Chipotle, a burrito chain, would make much sense. Sharon Zackfia, an analyst at William Blair, said that it would entail “no obvious revenue synergies,” beyond weird mash-ups like offering customers, say, a grande Iced Pumpkin Cream Shaken Espresso with their guac-laden burrito bowl. “Investors would likely heavily question the rationale and whether such a move signals that management has less confidence in Starbucks’ future growth prospects,” she said in a note, per the FT. Because OpenAI is a private company, it’s important to note that we don’t know for sure what’s going on under the hood. That said, the FT based its reporting on internal memos to investors issued in late September. Basically, the AI lab told backers to expect revenues approaching $50 billion — far shorter than the explosive $70 billion number reported previously. Though that $70 billion figure was also based on leaked investor memos, OpenAI wasn’t in any hurry to deny it, digging itself into a $20 billion hole. Given the monumental difference between those two figures, investors across the world of finance are taking it as a sign that demand for AI is much lower than previously thought — a signal with ripple effects that extend far beyond OpenAI. At the closing bells on Wall Street on Thursday, the tech-heavy Nasdaq index was down a whopping 1.25 percent on the day, while the S&P 500 fell by 0.47 percent. Particularly hard hit were companies sandwiched in the middle of the AI supply-chain, like the chipmaker Nvidia, which tumbled by nearly 3 percent following the news from the FT. All of this comes a week after consulting firm Bain and Co issued a gloomy report finding that the global AI industry would have to reach annual revenue of $6 trillion by 2031 to justify the amount of spending on AI data centers — a formidable summit, which is now $20 billion farther after the latest disclosures. Don’t Want to Spend Time With Your Kids? Train an AI Voice Model of Yourself to Read Them Bedtime Stories

Futurism | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Parenting is hard. Why not let an AI model take over some of your childrearing duties? It may come at the expense of forming lasting emotional bonds with your progeny, but that’s a tradeoff that some AI adherents, evidently, have calculated to be worth it. OpenAI Sam Altman has bragged about how much he relies on ChatGPT to raise his son, and also thinks it’s a good idea to let an AI-generated podcast replace the “so how’d your day go?” catchup talk a mom or dad has in the car when they pick their kid up from school. Altman’s comments were ruthlessly — and rightfully — mocked at the time. But his robo-rearing ideas pale in comparison to this latest take from an AI bro. In a now deleted post on X, Alvin Sng, a software engineer at the startup Factory AI, recalled a conversation he had with his wife when she asked him to read his newborn baby a book. As he explained, he had a better idea that required zero effort on his part. “I can just play an audiobook from my phone,” he told her. His spouse reasonably complained that an audiobook wouldn’t be in his voice. But Sng wasn’t to be outdone in the zero-effort-parenting department. “I can train a voice model,” he countered, before addressing his readers: “Parenting hits different when you work in AI.” Folks, what is the p(doom) of becoming estranged from your kid? A baby can’t understand what a father is reading it, but it can recognize his voice. There’s something perverse about some of a baby’s early and formative exposure to what its father sounds like being an AI approximation. And already wanting to automate parenting duties this early on doesn’t bode well for the future. The precious time between a child and a parent doesn’t need to be streamlined, but the AI industry is obsessed with selling us on the idea of optimizing every part of our lives. Let an AI like Meta’s buzzy Muse agent send texts and emails on your behalf. Let an AI plan out your day or plan out your vacation. Let it handle bookings and reservations. Let it read for you and also let it be your friend, since human friends aren’t always available and sometimes disagree with you. The response from netizens to Sng’s story was one of horror and disbelief. “Shouldn’t your first instinct be to read your baby a book willingly as a father and to spend more time with your baby?” asked one user. Another called it “one of the saddest things I’ve read on this app.” Jess Calarco, a sociologist and author, called it “telling that men would rather try to invent tech to replace humans’ role in caregiving than step up as equal partners in parenting.” Many parents, all the same, are embracing AI. Some placate their kids by letting them talk endlessly with ChatGPT voice mode. One mom even built an AI clone of herself so her son could talk to it while she traveled for work. Sensing demand, Meta is building an AI app called “StoryKit” that dreams up and reads personalized bedtime stories for children. For Sng, AI was on the brain the moment his child came kicking and screaming into this world. Before his controversial post, he uploaded a photo with his wife and baby at the hospital with an eyebrow raising caption. “After spawning so many agents,” he wrote, “it was about time to spawn a human.” More on AI: AI Is Creating “Doom Loops” All Over the Economy

We can’t help treating AI like it’s human. But should we?
2026-10-09

We can’t help treating AI like it’s human. But should we?

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. In a tan polo shirt, brown leather belt, and round bolo tie, MIT PhD student Wil Norton kind of looks like a park ranger, leading our tour group through a tropical jungle. We’re not actually on safari, though — we’re at MIT’s Computer Science and Artificial Intelligence Lab (CSAIL). But what we’re about to see is even more exotic than a cheetah or a jaguar: a pair of humanoid Unitree robots, which each cost about $50,000. (“They’ll tell you they cost $16,000,” Norton says. “That’s a lie.”) We usually only see these robots on YouTube when they’re performing a synchronized martial arts routine, or dancing on “America’s Got Talent.” Here, they’re approachable. Even though I know it isn’t sentient, I want to be its friend. With Norton’s permission, I approach the robot, whose nickname is Gabe. For a moment, I’m not sure how to interact with something that has a LiDAR sensor where its brain should be, but I default to treating it like a person. “Hello, sir, it’s nice to meet you,” I say, grabbing its gloved, dextrous hand and shaking it. I think about this interaction later — my instinctive impulse to be friendly to a robot — when I attend Dr. Sherry Turkle‘s “Artificial Intimacy” book launch at MIT Future Fest. Turkle, who joined the faculty at MIT in 1976, has been researching the relationship between humans and computers perhaps longer than anyone, charting technology’s psychological impact on us from the first home computers to ChatGPT. “When we are drawn into even the most primitive exchanges with a relational artifact, we believe it cares for us,” Turkle writes in the book. “And we are wired to care for it in return.” I’m not alone in my tendency to anthropomorphize robots and large language models (LLMs) — as Turkle notes, it seems to be in our nature. One study found that about 70% of people are polite when interacting with AI, while another showed that users are more likely to say “please” or “thank you” to chatbots once a conversation has progressed past a first prompt-and-response. “People tend to categorize whether this is the right use or wrong use, but I don’t think that is a clear boundary,” Dr. Pat Pataranutaporn, a colleague of Turkle who founded the Cyborg Psychology research group at the MIT Media Lab, told TechCrunch. “The question is, who are we becoming when we talk to [AI]?” There’s no material consequence of my politeness toward Gabe the robot, but as LLMs infiltrate everything from Google searches to customer service interactions, it has become routine for people to interact with these seemingly sentient chatbots. We’re more vulnerable than ever to the flip side of this impulse, which is that some people can ascribe so much legitimacy to the feelings of chatbots — which are programmed to never spark conflict — that they end up favoring them over other humans. “You can think of it like fantasy and fiction. We go to the cinema even though we know the story is completely fictional, but we want to experience a certain dimension of human experience that maybe our reality cannot give us,” Pataranutaporn said. “How do we benefit from that ability to experience other dimensions of human experience without losing it to the fantasy, where you can no longer come back to the reality?” Pataranutaporn describes himself as a “critical optimist,” and he studies AI’s potential to contribute to human flourishing. But he’s well aware of how dangerous this technology can be in the extremes. He even served as an expert in a wrongful death lawsuit over the suicide of 14-year-old Sewell Setzer, III, whose mother sued the chatbot platform Character.AI, alleging that a chatbot modeled after a “Game of Thrones” character had encouraged her son to end his life. “It can create a situation where people don’t feel agency in actually creating their own future — like when you say that AI will replace everyone, people ask, what are we gonna do? And these companies are not really responding ethically or responsibly,” Pataranutaporn said. “How do we have imagination while still being responsible for the impact of that imagination?” While Turkle’s book explores the risk of turning to AI for emotional support, rather than other people, she doesn’t think that AI-human interactions are universally harmful. She recalls a project she advised in which a student created an AI bot to help people practice for job interviews. “My big metaphor is, stay in your lane, chatbot!” Turkle said at the event. “Coaching for a job interview? That’s a lane. I can do that… A chatbot that says to a three-year-old, ‘I’m your parent, you can talk to me, not your parents,’ is not in a lane.” But when this technology deviates from its lane even slightly, we become vulnerable to the way it can slowly chip away at our skepticism and make us trust it. It can start with something as simple as a handshake. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Tesla renames ‘Full Self-Driving’ to ‘Tesla Assisted Driving’ in Europe

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Tesla has changed the name of its advanced driver assistance system in Europe after pushback from Germany’s transportation ministry. It’s now called “Tesla Assisted Driving,” instead of “Full Self-Driving (Supervised).” It’s a big capitulation from Tesla, which has been using the “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)” name for years despite the system requiring humans to pay attention to the road and take over when necessary. But the name change could help Tesla achieve its goal of wider adoption on the continent, which could unlock a potentially massive new revenue stream for the company. Germany’s transport minister, Steffen Bilger, said earlier this month that calling the software “Full Self-Driving” was somewhat misleading because it doesn’t perform the entire task of driving. He spoke with Tesla last month, and the company suggested the name change, according to an official release from the ministry. Bilger said he will now advocate for approval of Tesla Assisted Driving across Europe ahead of an EU-wide vote that could happen later this year. Tesla CEO Elon Musk thanked Bilger on X this week. Musk has spent years agitating for Full Self-Driving (Supervised), or FSD, to be approved in Europe. “FSD in Europe will save lives of people in the car, as well as vulnerable road users like pedestrians, cyclists, and pets,” he wrote on X in June 2025. “Please let your government know that you want approval.” In March of this year, he wrote that the “extreme regulatory burden” of the EU “stifles innovation in Europe,” adding that Europe “will love Tesla self-driving!” Just a few weeks later, the Netherlands became the first European country to approve FSD (Supervised). On Wednesday, Reuters reported that Tesla’s campaign for European approval has been “built on faulty company safety research” and “flawed studies.” The outlet also reported that the Dutch vehicle safety regulator, RDW, faced fierce pressure from Tesla and its legion of online supporters. Tesla’s software has certainly become more capable over the years. What used to be little more than a mix of lane centering and adaptive cruise control is now a driver assistance system that some owners use for nearly 100% of their daily driving. FSD can pull in and out of parking spots and navigate surface streets and highways. Tesla is so confident in the technology that it recently made it possible for FSD to intervene in a human driver’s control of the vehicle if it senses an oncoming collision — even if FSD is not active. But FSD has been at the center of federal investigations and civil lawsuits over crashes in which it was allegedly in use. Its less capable predecessor, Autopilot, has been associated with dozens of fatalities. Tesla discontinued Autopilot earlier this year after a judge ruled Tesla had engaged in deceptive marketing about the system’s capabilities. Remember Orkut? Its founder wants to bring it back
2026-10-09

Remember Orkut? Its founder wants to bring it back

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Orkut, the once-popular social networking platform that shut down more than a decade ago, may finally be making a comeback, as its founder has called on users to help revive it with a renewed focus on friendship, community, and human connection. In a post on X on Thursday, Orkut Büyükkökten, the founder of the eponymous social network, urged users to visit Orkut.com and add their names in support of bringing it back, as well as invite others to join. Orkut first debuted in 2004 as a side project by Büyükkökten, 51, while he was at Google. The social network went on to become hugely popular in India and Brazil but later lost ground to Facebook. Google eventually shut it down in September 2014, shifting its focus to other platforms, including Google+ and YouTube. In a letter published on Orkut.com, Büyükkökten criticized how social media has evolved over the past two decades, particularly the growing influence of algorithms and AI-generated content. “Artificial intelligence is extraordinary technology, but it’s dissolving the one premise that made social networking special and worth building: that there was a person on the other side,” he wrote. “I know how to get there: we rebuild the internet we first loved. The one with authentic people, real connection, and genuine belonging,” Büyükkökten added. This is not the first time Büyükkökten has explored a return to social networking. In 2016, two years after Orkut’s shutdown, he launched another social network, Hello, focused on connecting people through shared interests. The platform shut down in 2020 after attracting more than 2 million downloads worldwide, compared with Orkut’s about 300 million active users. In 2022, Büyükkökten hinted at another social networking venture in a note on Orkut.com, saying that he was “building something new.” He did not offer further details. Two years later, he told WIRED he was working on a social network that would “leverage AI and machine learning” to bring people together and build communities. That platform has yet to launch. Now, Büyükkökten is asking people to register their support for Orkut’s return. He has, however, not clarified whether a new version of the platform is already in development or whether it will ever launch. An email sent to Orkut seeking comment from Büyükkökten did not elicit a response. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. a16z’s Olivia Moore on the state of consumer AI
2026-10-09

a16z’s Olivia Moore on the state of consumer AI

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. I wrote this week about the bleak economics of consumer AI — but where some see a crisis, others see an opportunity. Olivia Moore covers consumer AI as a partner at Andreessen Horowitz — and on Monday, she released a report on the top 100 consumer AI apps. ChatGPT is still the biggest player by a mile, but smaller players like Suno and ElevenLabs are showing real staying power. Even more interesting is what we don’t see, as Moore’s report details half a dozen consumer categories that seem to be completely untouched by AI. Moore sees a huge opportunity in consumer AI, particularly if the industry can tap into revenue streams beyond just subscriptions and API charges. On a call this week, I talked with her about the unusual economics of consumer AI and why it’s still early days for the category. This interview has been edited for length and clarity. It’s an interesting moment for a report about consumer AI, since we’ve seen OpenAI pivot back to enterprise this year. It’s inspired a lot of pessimism about the overall revenue picture for consumer AI, including from me. But I take it you’re more optimistic? I agree with you that OpenAI has moved back towards the enterprise. To me, it’s not necessarily a pivot because they’re still launching a lot in consumer. It’s more of an expansion. But I don’t blame them, as much as I love consumer, because almost all of the AI revenue thus far has come from subscriptions and then token usage. And that is much more concentrated on the enterprise and prosumer side. How can consumer AI products change that revenue problem? The State of Markets report reminded us that just 2.2% of U.S. households are paying for AI. Do we need that number to rise before the market becomes attractive? I actually don’t know if I want to see that user number increase. I’m more interested in getting back to a world where the consumer is monetized in a way that isn’t subscription dollars out of their own pocket. I think it’s very easy in Silicon Valley, with people who are high-income and have corporate cards to pay for those tools, to say, “oh well, I’d rather pay for the product.” But I think most people would actually rather have free access to something and see some ads, and then they can decide if they want to subscribe or not to make the ads go away. The other challenge is the marginal cost of AI services, which is still a lot higher than classic internet services like Facebook or Google Search. Do you see a lot of these services finding ways to keep those costs down, whether it’s open source or other lightweight models? Yeah, I think we’re seeing improvements there. Even ChatGPT now has a Go plan, which is just $8 a month, and I’m assuming it runs on cheaper models. We don’t necessarily need frontier intelligence for every task, and so that could mean cheaper models, especially for some consumer use cases. It could also mean more consumer companies building on top of open source models. We’ve heard a lot of that from our founders lately, and it really feels like it’s starting to change. The problem is that right now, the users driving the revenue are doing coding and other technical automation, where they probably do need frontier intelligence. I think as we get more people building for the consumer, where the model is not the product, we’ll start to see lower-cost models used more often. It’s unusual to talk about technical automation in a consumer service, which feels like another unique feature of AI. Are we starting to blur the lines between consumer and enterprise? I wrote an article about this maybe a year or so ago called The Great Expansion trying to get at this. Pre-AI, you would have companies like Canva that were consumer-first and often took six or seven years to even add teams or enterprise plans. Now we’re seeing companies like Gamma, ElevenLabs, and Cursor that start as consumer and within 18 months become majority-enterprise businesses. Almost all of what we think of as consumer AI, I would argue, is prosumer AI. This really comes out on the revenue list, where there’s basically like three categories that power users are spending on. There’s product-building apps, so like Lovable, Replit, and Fal. There’s product marketing, which is AI ad generators like Higgsfield or HeyGen. Then there’s general work management, like Manus, Fireflies AI, and Granola. Those are like being paid for by consumers to start with, but I would argue that they’re not consumer in the way that we would think about pre-AI. There are also all these whitespace categories: social apps, dating apps, marketplaces, retail, travel, finance, health. There are no entrants in our top 100 list in those categories, which is pretty surprising. And that’s what I think we need to see over the next six months. So maybe we haven’t really seen true consumer AI yet? When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Automattic loses its interim CFO just weeks after boardroom shakeup

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Automattic’s interim chief financial officer Jeremy Klaperman has left his position after less than a month, TechCrunch has learned. Klaperman had been elevated to the new job last month after the governance crisis at the WordPress.com parent company, which saw its entire board either quit or be fired. The company confirmed the departure with the following statement, shared by Automattic’s director of communications Megan Fox: “Automattic will always be grateful for Jeremy Klaperman’s leadership. We wish him the best of luck on his next endeavor.” Before serving as the organization’s interim finance chief, Klaperman had served as the CFO of Automattic’s VIP Enterprise business unit. He had replaced the former CFO, Mark Davies, who had briefly become Automattic’s CFO after the company’s board members voted to put CEO Matt Mullenweg on leave in September. Within 33 hours of the board’s decision, Mullenweg retook the CEO role, and the board was wiped clean. Automattic’s chief legal officer Andy Missan had also left the company alongside Davies. Both executives had signed reciprocal severance deals during Mullenweg’s short leave of absence, TechCrunch previously reported. Automattic’s new board of directors was announced in late September, and includes best-selling science-fiction author Hugh Howey; two co-founders of the now-defunct social app IRL, Henry Khachatryan and Krutal Desai; plus author Amy Chan. It’s unclear why Klaperman chose to leave Automattic, but sources told TechCrunch that the decision came after he was demoted back to his original role at VIP. (As an interim CFO, he could have been replaced by a permanent hire, but nothing on that front has yet to be announced.) Automattic’s spokesperson did not comment on the reason for Klaperman’s departure. When Mullenweg originally announced Klaperman’s new role as interim CFO on Automattic’s Slack, Mullenweg reiterated that he had “100% confidence” in the company’s cash and financial position. Who Is Angela Paxton? Ken Paxton’s Estranged Wife Wades Into Texas Senate Battle—Without Mentioning Him

Who Is Angela Paxton? Ken Paxton’s Estranged Wife Wades Into Texas Senate Battle—Without Mentioning Him

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Texas State Sen. Angela Paxton (R-McKinney) speaks on-stage during the Turning Point USA Young Women's Leadership Summit at the Gaylord Texan Resort and Convention Center on June 14, 2025 in Grapevine, Texas. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Angela Paxton, the estranged wife of Texas Attorney General and Republican Senate candidate Ken Paxton, has waded into the state’s hotly contested Senate race by encouraging Texans to vote Republican in the midterm elections—while avoiding directly mentioning her husband. Angela Paxton—who filed for divorce last year, alleging adultery—long remained mum on the race, in which her husband is facing off against Democrat James Talarico. Over the summer, she declined to share with the New York Times whether she would vote for Ken Paxton in the November election. But as Talarico continues to hold a slim lead over her husband in most polls and Election Day draws closer, she has been speaking out to urge voters to back the GOP when they head to the voting booths next month. In a speech to a group of Republican women in mid-September, she said that the Republican Party has “some vulnerabilities on our ballot,” but urged them to still vote for the party’s candidates “even if we’re mad at it.” “I’ll be honest with you, I think Republican women will decide this election, because some of those vulnerabilities, I think, have been particularly alienating to women that hold our values,” she said. “When I say we have to win, I mean we have to win all those races. We have to keep all of those seats.” “It’s not the people—it’s the seat,” she continued. “Because we have more control over that seat and the future when we control the seat, when it’s still in the family, even if we’re mad at it. But if we lose it to the other team, the nuances that we want to fix with maybe a certain candidate are nothing compared to what it’s going to take to get it back.” Read More: How Trump Helped Put a Texas Senate Seat In Play And she is now sharing a similar message in a new ad, in which she talks about, in her own words, “the elephant in the room.” “People have asked my thoughts about this election and it turns out, I have some. Let’s talk about the elephant in the room,” Angela Paxton opens the ad by saying. “In a general election, it’s not as much about the individual players on the team—it’s about getting the whole team across the finish line,” she says. “Either we hold every Republican seat or we hand the future of Texas to the other team. That’s why I’m voting for the Republican ticket on Nov. 3. Let’s hold the line together.” She does not speak about Ken Paxton or the Senate contest outright in the ad. The ad was funded by Texas PAC, which is tied to a super PAC that backs a Republican majority in the upper chamber. Here’s what to know about Angela Paxton, and the significance of her wading into the midterm elections. Angela Paxton, a Republican, has served in the Texas state Senate since 2019. She was previously a math teacher and school counselor. She and Ken Paxton have been married for nearly four decades, but last year, she announced that she filed for divorce “on biblical grounds,” stating that, “in light of recent discoveries, I do not believe that it honors God or is loving to myself, my children, or Ken to remain in the marriage.” In her divorce petition, Angela Paxton alleged that her husband “has committed adultery” and said that the two of them had stopped living together “as spouses” by June 2024. A spokesperson for the Texas state senator told the Times over the weekend that the divorce proceedings are still underway. Political experts tell TIME that the ad featuring Angela Paxton suggests that the Republican Party is worried about her husband’s chances of winning the Senate race. “The fact that the need was felt to reach out to [Texas state senator Angela Paxton] to obliquely vouch for her now estranged husband is pretty remarkable, and an indication of the extent of concern that Republicans are feeling in this race,” says Joshua Blank, the research director of the Texas Politics Project at the University of Texas at Austin. Most polls show that Paxton is trailing—by a very slim margin—behind Talarico. If Talarico wins the Senate race, it would be the first time that a Democratic candidate has won a statewide election in Texas in more than three decades. Paxton’s campaign has been shadowed by several scandals and controversies that have arisen throughout his political career, including allegations of infidelity, a fraud indictment, and an impeachment trial on charges of bribery and abuse of office. Paxton has denied any wrongdoing. The fraud charges against him were ultimately dropped after prosecutors and Paxton agreed to a deal, and he was acquitted of all charges against him in his impeachment trial by the Texas state Senate. Political experts say that President Donald Trump’s low approval ratings—particularly how voters feel about his handling of the economy—could also be impacting Paxton’s performance in the polls, as well as an unusually tight gubernatorial race in Texas. “Nationwide, we know it’s a difficult environment for Republicans. That’s playing out in Texas too, though Texas is more Republican than most states and so there’s more of a buffer,” says Kirby Goidel, a professor of political science at Texas A&M University. According to data shared by CNN Chief Data Analyst Harry Enten on air earlier this month, Paxton’s net popularity is the lowest among several Republicans vying for key Senate seats next month. “[Paxton is] in a tight race like other Republicans, but he’s in a tighter race in a worse situation because, I think, of the baggage that he brings to the campaign,” Goidel says. Goidel says that, if you’re the Republican Party looking at these polls, “you say, what does Ken Paxton have to do to win this race?” “Well, the first thing he has to do is secure Republican support,” Goidel continues. “I think having his wife come on and send that message that this is the team game, not an individual game, and we need all the players on the field, is a … signal [from the party of] ‘Hey, we need you all to come on board and vote for this guy; you may not like it, but we need you.’” Blank also says that the ad featuring Angela Paxton “is not so indirectly addressed at giving women, but in particular Republican women who might have apprehensions about supporting Ken Paxton, with permission to do so.” A New York Times/Siena University poll conducted at the end of September found that more men who are likely voters in Texas said they would vote for Paxton than women who are likely voters in the state, at 51% compared to 40%. Blank points out, though, that there’s a gender gap in partisanship in American politics more generally—women tend to be more likely than men to align themselves with the Democratic Party, while men tend to be more likely than women to align themselves with the Republican Party. Blank notes that “polling has indicated a consistent gap in enthusiasm among Republican voters.” “Republican women in particular might be a soft spot in the coalition, and if anything, this ad indicates that that’s likely the case,” Blank says.
