On the Ground at the Power to the People Festival, a Day of Protest Music and Solidarity
2026-10-09

On the Ground at the Power to the People Festival, a Day of Protest Music and Solidarity

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Bruce Springsteen, Tom Morello, Joan Baez, and others appear onstage for the finale of the 2026 Power To The People Festival on Oct. 03, 2026 in Columbia, Md. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Tom Morello has been staging benefit shows since before Rage Against the Machine made him an icon: $10 at the door, proceeds to a homeless shelter, guitars loud enough to wage a sonic revolt against the establishment. Last year, while serving as musical director of Back to the Beginning, the Birmingham farewell concert for Ozzy Osbourne and Black Sabbath, he began imagining something bigger: a multigenerational, multigenre festival built on his foundational proposition that music can be an act of resistance—aimed squarely at Donald Trump and the forces he has unleashed. "It's no accident that the concert was about three weeks out from the midterms," Morello told me after the Oct. 3 Power to the People show at Merriweather Post Pavilion in Columbia, Md., less than 30 minutes from the capital whose balance of power those elections will decide. "In a time where hatred and division are rife, this is a festival of love and solidarity. In a time where injustice is rampant, this is a festival celebrating those who are standing up against injustice. And in a time where artists are either being canceled or censoring themselves, it was a guarantee the artists were going to be able to play what they wanted to play and say what they wanted to say." To that end, Morello assembled a sprawling roster of rebels and rock-n-roll royalty: Joan Baez and Public Enemy; Jack Black and the Dropkick Murphys; Cypress Hill and Nathaniel Rateliff; Killer Mike and Serj Tankian; Stephen Stills and Dave Matthews and the Foo Fighters. Headlining the event was Bruce Springsteen, fresh off a 20-show tour that had become a nightly rebuke of Trump, MAGA, and the authoritarian currents he saw threatening the American experiment. Power to the People suggested a rebellious energy stirring at a moment when many artists had grown wary of speaking out, fearful of backlash, of alienating part of their audience, of the wrath of an administration that has repeatedly targeted prominent critics. "I feel as though we're trying to turn the tide and we're facing a tsunami," said Baez, who sang alongside Bob Dylan and Pete Seeger during the protest movements of the 1960s and '70s. Then she turned to the crowd. "You are the antidote in a world of cruelty and bullying." With Rateliff beside her, she launched into a haunting rendition of Woody Guthrie's "Deportee." Inevitably, the place felt like a progressive family reunion: aging boomers who carry the counterculture as a kind of secular inheritance; younger activists animated by rage against corporate power, democratic erosion, and the war in Gaza; keffiyehs, protest shirts, and homemade placards scattered across the grounds. One read, Keep the Immigrants. Deport the Racists. MS NOW livestreamed the proceedings. It would be easy to dismiss the show as an exercise in preaching to the converted. But movements have to begin somewhere, and politics is downstream of culture. If the message travels beyond the lawn, Power to the People may come to represent one of the seminal cultural events of the era: a gathering of artists, at a liminal moment in American life, turning their influence into a political insurgency to preserve the nation's democratic future. A shot of the stage at the Power to the People Festival Paul Morigi—Getty Images Morello had been planning the festival for more than a year, but he says the "turning point" came after the Operation Metro Surge, when Trump administration deployed roughly 3,000 federal officers to Minneapolis this winter for the largest immigration-enforcement operation in the country, which culminated in the killings of two Americans, Renee Good and Alex Pretti. "When you're putting on a show like this, managers and agents are not always eager to get involved," Morello told me. But after Minneapolis, something changed. "Artists started calling me up and raising their hand, saying, 'I understand you're putting on a festival. Can I be involved?'" One was Ken Casey, frontman of the Dropkick Murphys, who tore through a 12-song set that ended with the band's signature anthem, "I'm Shipping Up to Boston," built around lyrics by Guthrie. "Days like today let us know that there's a community out there that wants to see a peaceful America, an empathetic America, a unified America, that wants to see control of our government given back to the people," Casey told me. The concert began at one in the afternoon and ran for 10 hours, the history of American protest music compressed into a single day. During Morello's early-evening set, Tankian from System of a Down joined him for Audioslave's "Like a Stone," a tribute to Morello's late bandmate Chris Cornell. Macklemore, recently dropped as an opener on Ed Sheeran's tour because of pressure from New England Patriots owner Robert Kraft over the artist’s pro-Palestinian advocacy, had been invited but was unavailable. So he sent a video message that played on the giant screens. The controversy itself reinforced the festival's defiant argument—rebelling against a world where speaking out still carries a price. The lineage grew explicit as the night wore on. Baez, Rateliff, and Matthews joined for "Deportee," then moved into the civil-rights standard "Ain't Gonna Let Nobody Turn Me Around." As they left the stage, Stephen Stills appeared and reached back to another era of upheaval with Buffalo Springfield's "For What It's Worth." Cypress Hill followed, then Public Enemy with their landmark "Fight the Power," then Dave Matthews. The Foo Fighters opened their set with "Times Like These," a song born of uncertainty and near-fracture for the band that transformed into an anthem of renewal. They closed with "Everlong." a track about the desire to make a perfect moment last forever, despite knowing that it can’t. But the concert’s climax was Springsteen's set. At 76, he summoned the power and energy that have defined him over a half century, as he occupies yet another role now, too: the elder statesman among a generation of musicians deciding how much to risk by taking a stand. "When you have a guy that's at that stage of his life, he's done all the stuff, he's comfortable, he didn't have to go on the road and do that and put the crosshairs on himself, but he did it anyway," Casey said. "If artists can't take a lead from that, then my God, nobody's paying attention." Springsteen opened with "Streets of Minneapolis," his protest song against Operation Metro Surge, written days after ICE flooded Minnesota's streets and murdered two protestors. He then brought Baez onstage for a searing acoustic rendition of Dylan’s "Chimes of Freedom.” From there the set erupted into anthemic rock fueled by righteous anger. He ripped through the Clash's "Clampdown" with Morello, then "The Ghost of Tom Joad," rekindling the version the two had performed on his recent Land of Hope and Dreams tour. Grohl and Matthews returned to the stage, and Springsteen's set became an ensemble of generations of American rock musicians gathered around the protest-song tradition. Together they tore into two classics of the canon, Creedence Clearwater Revival's "Fortunate Son" and Neil Young's "Rockin' in the Free World." When the last chords faded, they stayed onstage for a final coda as the rest of the day’s performers joined them onstage. Baez and Springsteen duet during the concert Paul Morigi—Getty Images The festival was a reincarnation of the spirit of Guthrie, Seeger, and the musicians who advocated for working-class dignity and civil rights in the mid-twentieth century. Morello stepped forward to explain what they were about to sing—and why: Guthrie’s "This Land Is Your Land," but with the verses often omitted from popular renditions, in which the narrator encounters a "Private Property" sign and Americans standing hungry outside a relief office. The lines restored the song's original edge—an unofficial patriotic anthem that also challenges the country to make good on its promise that America belongs to everyone. It was the version Springsteen sang with Seeger at Barack Obama's inaugural concert in January 2009, and one he resurrected last spring at Music America: The Songs That Shape Us, his concert at Monmouth University celebrating the nation's 250th anniversary, where he recruited the kind of star-level talent the President had been unable to marshal to Washington for the occasion. For Morello, the song also evoked his mother, Mary Morello, who recently died at 102. A child of the Great Depression, she possessed an activist spirit, he said, feeding the homeless, helping recovering addicts earn their GEDs, and supporting her son's early punk bands. "Her fingerprints were all over this," Morello told me. "Her DNA was in every bit of this. I don't think it was any accident that this concert happened in a place called Mary Land." It was also there, of course, because of its proximity to the White House, and so they closed with John Lennon's "Power to the People." Morello, Springsteen, and company wanted Trump to hear them, at a precarious moment for the President and the MAGA movement, with the midterms weeks away and his poll numbers at their lowest point of his second term. But the festival's message was ultimately a call to agency, a reminder that in a democracy it is the voters who get to determine their destiny. "History is not something that happens," Morello told the crowd. "History is something we make."

Your Favorite Song Might Make Pain Hurt Less
2026-10-09

Your Favorite Song Might Make Pain Hurt Less

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings No one much likes the pain of a needle stick at a doctor’s office or hospital. But according to a new study in Frontiers in Pain Research, there’s one thing that can ease the sting: music. Listening to just the right soundtrack may be its own kind of anesthetic—one that could be useful for needle insertions and other painful medical procedures. “Although a needle puncture is a minor procedure, the level of pain can vary significantly from person to person,” said radiologist and lead author Dr. Andrei-Cristinel Dragnea of University Hospital Zürich, in a statement that accompanied the release of the study. “In modern medicine, the goal of treating physicians is not only to provide a successful diagnosis and therapy, but also to ensure that the procedure causes as little discomfort as possible.” To conduct their research, Dragnea and his colleagues studied 204 people who were undergoing an MRI of the brain, spine, head, or neck, and divided them into four groups. One group listened to soft relaxation music similar to what’s played during a massage; one listened to a Mozart composition; one listened to their personal favorite songs; and the fourth, a control group, did not listen to music. For the first three groups, the music was played before, during, and after the insertion of a cannula, which is a port-like tube slid into a vein for the purpose of administering medication, general anesthetic, or contrast dye before an MRI. The Mozart music was not just any selection, but Sonata K488, written for two pianos, in D major. This song made headlines in 1993 when a study published in the journal Nature found that listening to classical music—and that composition in particular—was linked to improved memory, problem-solving, and spatial-temporal reasoning. Though the mechanism involved wasn’t firmly established, the finding gave rise to what became known in science as the "Mozart effect.” The songs chosen by the group that listened to their favorite tunes included, among others, AC/DC’s Safe in New York City, Lionel Richie’s Hello, and the Crash Test Dummies’ Mmm Mmm Mmm Mmm. People were asked to rate their pain before and after the cannula insertion. Those who listened to the soft relaxation music or no music had no significant pain reduction before, during, or after the procedure. There are possible reasons the lilting, generic music was ineffective. “Relaxation music is usually characterized by a slow speed and low volume, and does not show noticeable dynamics,” said Dr. Meritxell Garcia Alzamora, a neuroradiologist at the University Hospital Zürich and senior author of the study, in a statement. “These characteristics may have contributed to the patients disregarding relaxation music, which would explain the lack of pain relief.” The other two groups did show some pain relief after listening to music. Those who heard the Mozart composition and those who listened to their favorite songs experienced significant pain reduction two minutes after the procedure. The “favorite music” group even had a bonus pain-relief perk that no other group experienced: just 30 seconds after the procedure, they rated their pain lower than the other groups, compared to their baseline. Why does some music appear to have an analgesic effect? The researchers did not try to answer the question definitively, but their findings did raise some possibilities. Listening to music, they write in the study, may trigger the release of endorphins, chemical messengers that bind to opioid receptors to reduce or eliminate pain. Music may also calm the body down, with a resulting reduction of blood pressure, heart rate, anxiety, and stress—all of which may reduce pain. Being distracted by music may also engage the brain’s limbic system and prefrontal networks, activating pain mediation networks. Those neural regions are known to have a somewhat delayed effect, which could explain why people experienced relief two minutes after the needle. It’s possible, too, the researchers concede, that the music wasn’t what was responsible for reduced pain, but rather the presence of a friendly, distracting experimenter in the room. Going forward, the investigators plan to explore other types of classical music beyond Mozart that may have similar—or even superior—pain-relieving benefits. They also hope to determine if music can serve as an analgesic for more severe pain than needle sticks. There are a lot of pharmacological approaches in an anesthesiologist’s toolkit. Music—no-cost, easy, and absent risk—could be a valuable drug-free addition.

Fjord Details a Family’s Sudden Nightmare in Norway

Fjord Details a Family’s Sudden Nightmare in Norway

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Reinsve and Stan, top center and right, play the parents of an ultra-religious family caught at the center of a child protection case. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Romanian filmmaker Cristian Mungiu makes thoughtful movies about social issues that cross international borders: In 4 Months, 3 Weeks and 2 Days, a young Romanian woman risks her life for an illegal abortion. R.M.N. explores xeno-phobia in a small Transylvanian village, dissecting the complexity of spontaneous human impulses in the process. Mungiu’s films can spur us to think anew about issues we’d rather keep in the background, and they can at times be deeply moving. They can also feel dreary and instructive, in an eat-your-vegetables way. Mungiu’s Fjord, which won the Palme d’Or at Cannes earlier in the year, offers a healthy dose of very nutritious vegetables, and in places the story almost—almost—stretches the bounds of believability. But its actors, including Sebastian Stan and Renate Reinsve, as well as two young newcomers, make you believe in one family’s sudden nightmare. Do the authorities entrusted to protect our children always know what’s best for them? Fjord suggests, provocatively, that the answer may not always be yes. Mihai Gheorghiu (Stan), who is Romanian, and his Norwegian wife Lisbet (Reinsve) have just relocated with their five children from Romania to Norway, to be closer to Lisbet’s parents. Mihai and Lisbet are also deeply religious, and appear to be raising their children in a sometimes strict but undeniably loving home. One day, at the children’s new school, an instructor charged with protecting the students’ welfare notices a bruise on the shoulder of the eldest Gheorghiu daughter, Elia (Vanessa Ceban). The administrator takes swift action, and before Mihai and Lisbet can fully grasp what’s happening, their children—including their infant, who’s still breastfeeding—are whisked into foster homes. Their struggle to reunite their family involves risky twists and a great deal of frustrating bureaucracy. Stan and Reinsve in Fjord Courtesy of Neon Unless you know Norway’s judicial system, you might be tempted to wonder: Could one family really be broken up that fast, with so little evidence? Mungiu’s premise seems somewhat far-fetched, intensified for dramatic reasons, though it’s worth noting that his script is based on a real-life case in Norway. And recently, in the U.S., Child Protective Services interviewed the children of former Secretary of Transportation Pete Buttigieg, separating the children from their fathers for 24 hours, after receiving a tip alleging that they were at risk. The report was proved false, but the incident shows how easily a system designed to protect kids might be manipulated. Regardless, the plausibility of Fjord’s premise is academic in the context of the performances given by Stan and Reinsve. Reinsve (so terrific in 2025’s Sentimental Value) is resolutely unglamorous here, a dutiful wife and mother decked out in dowdy clothes. But her distress over the loss of her children crosses the planes of her face like a muted shadow; you sense some anger beneath that meek veneer. And Stan, who is Romanian American, gives layers of complexity to Mihai as a father figure. He clearly loves his kids, but he also has a temper. Stan doesn’t make Mihai easy to read, just as the movie around him doesn’t offer any easy conclusions. Yet amid all the child-custody drama Fjord lays before us, a subplot involving the deepening friendship between Elia and a neighbor couple’s daughter, Noora (Henrikke Lund-Olsen), ends up being the film’s most powerful and moving element. These two young teenagers aren’t just navigating a new friendship; they’re exploring feelings that may or may not be the roots of romantic love. Sometimes kids tell us what we need to know about life, even if it’s not always the lesson we expect or want. And sometimes they steal a movie away from the grownups. Fjord, perhaps unbeknownst even to the man who made it, really belongs to them.

China Wants to Sell The World on Xinjiang-Farmed Fish
2026-10-09

China Wants to Sell The World on Xinjiang-Farmed Fish

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When Chinese state television hailed inland salmon as a miracle in 2018, many viewers saw something more familiar: not a wonder, but a sales pitch. The segment, aired by China Central Television that May, celebrated salmon farming in western China. Over images of sleek fish cutting through clear water, the narrator marveled that “a precious fish like salmon, which typically lives in the open ocean,” could now be raised inland, in the country’s barren western hinterland. The message was unmistakable. Technology had made the improbable possible. “The land is the same land, and the people are the same people,” the program declared, “but when technology is brought in, miracles are born.” Online, viewers began poking at the claim almost immediately. Was that salmon at all, many asked, or just rainbow trout dressed up as something fancier? On Chinese social media, the mood turned from curiosity to irritation. “People expect Atlantic salmon, but we’re getting freshwater rainbow trout that costs half as much,” one Beijing-based WeChat account complained. “How could you not be angry?” Science writer Fang Shimin dispensed with niceties: “This is cheap rainbow trout being passed off as expensive salmon. If you don’t want to deceive consumers, just call it rainbow trout.” Such anxieties would only grow larger as China worked to sell an idea that, not long ago, might have sounded implausible: seafood from the desert. As China has rapidly expanded its fish farms as part of a state policy aimed at self-sufficiency, it has developed at least 1,000 fish farms in Xinjiang, a historically restive region located in the country’s mountainous and desert inland. The Outlaw Ocean Project, a news organization in Washington, D.C. that I founded and run, mapped these farms as part of a sweeping investigation showing how much seafood is reaching the U.S., including government facilities, in violation of the Uyghur Forced Labor Prevention Act (UFLPA). The distrust was not irrational. The United Nations’ Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) said this year that while there is no official global estimate for seafood fraud, empirical studies suggest it may exist in up to 20% of the worldwide trade, sometimes appearing as species substitution and other kinds of misrepresentation. For most people, seafood comes with an expected geography. Salmon belongs to Norway or Alaska. Cod evokes the cold North Atlantic. Shrimp suggests warm coasts and estuaries. Xinjiang, by contrast, calls up deserts, surveillance, cotton fields, and the Chinese government’s mass detention, forced-labor, and assimilation campaign against Uyghurs and other predominantly Muslim minorities, along with the long, hard distances of the country’s far west. The most landlocked patch on the planet, Xinjiang is among the last places many people would associate with premium fish. And yet for years, Chinese officials, state media, and seafood companies have been trying to teach consumers to do exactly that. They have promoted “Xinjiang seafood” as one of the small wonders of a new China: fish raised in glacial meltwater, in cold mountain reservoirs, in alkaline lakes, and at farms built against the edge of the desert. The imagery is always some variation on the same promise. Geography is no longer destiny. Harsh landscapes can be made productive. A remote frontier, disciplined by engineering, can become a source of delicacy and pride. In that sense, Xinjiang seafood is not merely an agricultural product. It is a narrative achievement. The state-led campaign to create that narrative matters because it offers a rare window into Beijing’s worries about food security and its methods for redirecting Western attention away from its human rights problems. It also showcases China’s larger ambitions of using industry to help settle a historically restive region. This campaign saw its zenith in 2023, when Xinhua News Agency reported that the “Xinjiang seafood” label had surged in prominence on Chinese social media. Government data from that year show Xinjiang as China’s second-largest producer of “salmon,” trailing only Qinghai Province on the Tibetan Plateau. Its success is also relevant to what Americans eat, as the Outlaw Ocean Project found that seafood from Xinjiang ends up in the U.S. in violation of federal law. One company, called Tianyun, now ships to 14 countries, including the U.S., and distributes to food companies that supply military bases, public schools, and U.S. congressional cafeterias, according to Bait to Plate, an online tool that tracks the fishing and seafood industries. Xinjiang Tianyun said in a statement that claims of systemic forced labor “are completely factually unfounded” and described the UFLPA as an unreasonable and politically motivated framework that violates the principles of fair international trade. Xinjiang Tianyun insists that it strictly abides by China’s laws on labor and contracts and resolutely protects the legitimate rights and interests of every employee. The 2018 salmon dispute exposed how fragile China's narrative of achievement in Xinjiang can be. To some consumers, the issue was simple: they feared they were paying salmon prices for a cheaper fish. But the outrage ran deeper because it also touched food safety. That same year, the Chinese food-safety expert Zhong Kai warned against serving trout raw, citing parasite risks common in freshwater fish. “I do not recommend eating it raw,” he said, advising thorough cooking. Online commenters accused state media of misleading the public. This was “not the salmon ordinary people think of,” they wrote. Masami Takeuchi, a food-safety officer at the FAO, noted that illnesses caused by eating raw freshwater fish can be serious, even when symptoms arrive late or are hard to trace. The fear, then, was not simply that consumers were being overcharged. It was that they were being sold an aspirational image that obscured material differences in the fish itself. That anxiety found ready soil in a country where food scandals have repeatedly taught the public to distrust official reassurance. China’s aquaculture industry has expanded at staggering speed, and with that expansion have come the familiar pressures of industrial animal farming: crowding, disease, waste, chemical intervention, and a recurring mismatch between the pastoral language used to market food and the industrial realities that produce it. A 2020 study in Nature found that aquaculture has the highest antibiotic-use intensity per kilogram of biomass among major animal-production sectors, exceeding use in both humans and land-based livestock. Chinese researchers have warned for years about severe antibiotic abuse in the industry. Sedatives have posed another problem. Diazepam, sometimes used to calm fish during transport, has repeatedly shown up in official inspections despite being prohibited. In 2021, China’s Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Affairs began requiring routine diazepam testing, and a 2023 paper in China Food Hygiene Journal described diazepam residues as “widely detected” in water and seafood products. In 2025, the U.S. Food and Drug Administration issued an alert after detecting residues of prohibited substances—including malachite green, a carcinogenic veterinary drug—in seafood shipments from China, citing “significant public-health concerns.” None of this proves that Xinjiang seafood as a whole is fraudulent or unsafe. But it helps explain why the category has struggled to inspire a durable sense of consumer trust. In a food system often shadowed by contamination scares and labeling disputes, grand claims end up feeling less like reassurance than marketing. The more insistently officials and companies promoted inland salmon, trout, whitefish, caviar, and other products from China’s far west as a new high-value frontier, the more some consumers wondered what, exactly, they were being asked to believe. The answer, in part, was a story the state had been helping to write for years. As early as 2011, Vice Minister of Agriculture Niu Dun was urging officials to “enhance brand building and awareness of the market expansion of Xinjiang’s seafood enterprises.” A 2017 government Action Plan for Fisheries Poverty Alleviation and Aid to Xinjiang and Tibet and Xinjiang went further, instructing local offices to “strengthen communication and coordination with mainstream media” and to “systematically publicize the progress of fishery poverty alleviation,” thereby creating what it called “an environment of positive public opinion.” The phrasing was telling. Fisheries in Xinjiang were to be built, yes. But they were also to be narrated. That narration took many forms. Officials promoted fish-themed tourism. In Wenquan County, a local attraction described as “Xinjiang’s first cold-water fish theme park” invited visitors to tour a museum, sample whitefish sashimi and caviar, and even catch fish on site, weigh them, and bring them into town for processing. The scene was faintly surreal: a region associated abroad with coercion and repression recast domestically as a place of family outings, provincial pride, and specialty delicacies. The politics were never absent. They were simply plated more attractively. Then, in late summer 2023, the campaign accelerated. Posts about Xinjiang seafood surged across Chinese social-media platforms. Hashtags such as “Xinjiang welcomes everyone to come eat seafood” and “No joke—Xinjiang really does produce seafood” climbed Weibo’s Hot Search List. On Douyin, the Chinese version of TikTok, interactions tied to Xinjiang seafood jumped spectacularly. A seafood industry report later said that videos associated with “Xinjiang seafood” and “Xinjiang salmon” amassed more than 150 million views in 2023. One widely circulated Douyin clip, “Xinjiang Salmon ‘Leaps’ onto the World Table,” drew more than 2.7 million views and helped touch off the frenzy. Like many of the videos that followed, it lingered on crystalline mountain water, gleaming facilities, and the visual incongruity of prized fish emerging from the country’s interior. Soon, the theme leapt beyond China’s own internet. YouTube, which is banned in China, saw a sharp rise in Xinjiang seafood videos that fall. The message was no longer meant only for domestic audiences. Xinjiang seafood was being staged for foreign consumption, too—not just as food, but as an emblem of innovation, abundance, and purity. The timing was hard to miss. On August 24, 2023, Japan began releasing into the Pacific Ocean treated wastewater that had accumulated since the Fukushima nuclear disaster. Beijing denounced the move as “selfish and irresponsible” and imposed a blanket ban on Japanese aquatic products. In the tense weeks that followed, content promoting Xinjiang seafood leaned heavily on purity: glacial melt from the Tian Shan, cold inland reservoirs, waters safely removed from allegedly contaminated seas. Xinjiang seafood was being sold not only as novel or upscale, but as patriotic, safe, and strategically desirable. To some analysts, the speed and scale of the online surge suggested something more coordinated than ordinary virality. Fergus Ryan, a senior analyst at the Australian Strategic Policy Institute, said the pattern bore the hallmarks of top-down and algorithmic amplification rather than grassroots enthusiasm alone. In his view, the campaign may also have served another purpose: redirecting attention from mounting scrutiny of China’s seafood sector after an October 2023 investigation by the Outlaw Ocean Project reported that seafood plants in Shandong had used thousands of Uyghur labor transfers to process fish destined for major Western markets. Against that backdrop, Ryan argued, the flood of upbeat Xinjiang seafood content functioned as a kind of information flooding, crowding search and recommendation systems with stories of “fishery miracles” rather than forced labor. Whether every post was centrally directed matters less than the political usefulness of the category itself. Xinjiang seafood offered the state an unusually supple symbol. It could suggest scientific prowess, dietary upgrading, regional development, ecological management, and ethnic harmony all at once. It could showcase “clean” water, advanced facilities, and smiling workers. Most of all, it could recast one of the world’s most scrutinized frontiers as a landscape of abundance. That ambition extends beyond domestic image-making. Beijing plainly hopes to export not just Xinjiang seafood but the idea of Xinjiang seafood. Yet that effort faces an obvious obstacle: In the United States, the UFLPA presumes that goods made wholly or partly in Xinjiang are produced with forced labor unless importers can prove otherwise, while in Europe, a 2024 forced-labor regulation will bar products made with forced labor from the E.U. market once it begins applying in December 2027. This is where the events depart from a conventional tale of food fraud. In many countries, misleading labels thrive because supply chains are opaque and enforcement is weak. In Xinjiang, opacity was part of the business model. The product itself was being woven into a larger political narrative about what the region was and what it was becoming. Fish farms were not merely farms. They were exhibits. Part of the effort to promote Xinjiang seafood involved government-orchestrated media tours for foreign journalists and vloggers, often called “frontier influencers” or “global stringers,” to visit Xinjiang, including its fish farms. After being shepherded to government-run facilities and through regions like Tibet or Xinjiang that are typically off limits to most other journalists, these influencers tend to create seeing-is-believing style videos that offer “happy life” testimonials from locals, while showcasing the region as a high-tech and indispensable hub for global development and “green” innovation. By 2025, Xinjiang seafood had become a recurring stop on that circuit. A state-funded outlet released a video featuring an American vlogger identified as Jack exploring what it called the “agricultural miracle” of a fish farm on the edge of China’s largest desert, the Taklamakan. That same month, a British creator toured a reservoir in Alar City and praised its role in both flood control and local fish production. A Russian student, one of the better-known foreign influencers working this terrain, filmed herself at a salmon farm in Nilka, announcing cheerfully that she had come to be “an employee for a day” and see the industry for herself. Some of the footage circulating during this push featured Tianyun, one of the companies caught up in the earlier inland salmon labeling dispute. Even some of the industry figures tied to those disputes were later recast as patriotic exemplars of frontier development. In 2025, one prominent executive from Xinjiang’s salmon business was honored in Beijing by the Communist Party as a “National Model Worker,” then pledged to help expand China’s salmon industry and improve the skills of workers from all ethnic groups. The symbolism was familiar by then. Commercial actors were being folded into a larger state story about modernization, unity, and progress in the far west. All of which points to the larger feat behind Xinjiang seafood’s rise. China did not simply have to learn how to raise cold-water fish in unlikely places. It had to construct a category and teach consumers how to feel about it. It had to make fish from a landlocked, desert-heavy, politically burdened region seem not only possible but desirable: clean rather than contaminated, premium rather than ersatz, modern rather than improvised, harmonious rather than coercive. That required hatcheries, reservoirs, feed, transport networks, and technical expertise. But it also required softer definitions, confident reassurances, staged tourism, influencer testimony, and a steady current of triumphal images. In the end, Xinjiang seafood is best understood not simply as a product of ponds, pipes, hatcheries, and feed, but as a product of narrative force. Fish flash through cold inland water; cameras glide across reservoirs; influencers smile into the desert light; officials speak of innovation and harmony. The image holds. Until, somewhere behind it, the old questions return: What is this fish, really? Where did it come from? What story is being wrapped around it? In Xinjiang, those questions do not fade when the meal arrives. They sit there at the table. (The reporting for this story was part of a larger investigation conducted by an international team of journalists based at The Outlaw Ocean Project. Ian Urbina is the founder and executive editor of The Outlaw Ocean Project.)

Rosalind Eleazar Gives One of the Year's Best Performances in the Acerbic and Tender Misty Green
2026-10-09

Rosalind Eleazar Gives One of the Year's Best Performances in the Acerbic and Tender Misty Green

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings It was probably around 100 years ago that stalwart Hollywood star Marie Dressler said, “You’re only as good as your last picture.” Today, that hard-nosed observation not only holds; in a culture measured out in here-today-gone-tomorrow limited series and independent films that fail to find their audience, it’s more apt, and more potentially heartbreaking, than ever. Misty Green, the title character and the heartbeat of Chris Rock’s fourth feature film as a director, is living out Dressler’s maxim. Misty, played by Rosalind Eleazar, is a gifted singer and actor who desperately needs a break. But there are good reasons she’s not going to get it. Even though she has an Emmy from her role in a hit series, she’s on her way to being forgotten. She has no patience for Hollywood BS and refuses to play the usual games. But she’s also prickly and unpleasant—she has alienated most of the people who might be able to help her—and a self-destructive streak hinders her further: She’d rather go out for a night of clubbing and coke than stay in and learn her lines for the next day’s important audition. So now she’s in desperate straits. Essentially homeless, she’s been camping out in the apartment of a rich—and dead—ex-lover, but the guy’s angry widow has just given her seven days to pack up and vacate. During all this, her fragile brother, Jay (Daniel Kaluuya), who has been diagnosed with bipolar disorder and whom she loves deeply, shows up at the flat that’s not even her own, needing more from her than she can provide. Rock and Eleazar in Misty Green Courtesy of A24 We’ve seen the story of the wannabe comeback kid before. And Misty Green isn’t always a thoroughly graceful film. One of the weaker links may be the character played by Rock himself, Jordan, a director who wants to help Misty but harbors amorous feelings that don’t feel wholly believable; Misty could use some help, but she may not need that kind of savior. But overall, Rock’s writing is sharp and perceptive, and he’s attuned not just to the horrors of trying to make it in Hollywood, but also to Misty’s specific plight—and to the reality that she’s at times almost repugnantly dislikable. Eleazar, best known for her role in Slow Horses, knows just how to shape this character without ever softening her. Misty is gifted, arrogant, impossible; at times, you begin to wonder if she even deserves a break. But Eleazar, giving one of the best performances of the year, tears our expectations to shreds: Who are we to decide what she deserves? At one point, during an audition, one of the men in charge of potentially hiring her blurts out, “Are you mixed?” and her eyes, moments ago dancing with fire, become glacier cold. Misty has made her way along a path paved with irrelevant, if not outright racist and sexist, questions and assumptions—it’s little wonder she can barely take any more. But even stronger than her resentment is her belief that she still has something to give. It may be a stereotype to characterize actors as needy people, but Eleazar captures an almost existential longing that goes beyond garden-variety neediness. And at her lowest moment, Misty’s desperation becomes nearly unbearable to watch. Eleazar brings us right to the knifepoint of her degradation, and it pierces us too. But Rock wouldn’t leave Misty in despair; he gives her the empathetic yet unsentimental ending she deserves. It’s almost impossible to like Misty, and yet, in a film that’s both acerbic and tender, Eleazar makes us love her, almost against our will. Hollywood, a place of dreams, can be inhumane to those who dream the biggest. Misty Green opens a safe space for them—without the false promise of a happy ending.

The fraud of firing 6000 workers and immediately hiring 6000 H-1Bs was too in your face. The Dept of Labor did the right thing by suspending Microsoft from the PERM program. H1B is/was intended for worker shortages: for roles you couldn't fill domestically. It was not supposed to decrease the wage of the role, but (in theory) keep it at parity. It was always a ruse, but that was the premise. If you're firing people and then hiring others at a fraction of the pay, you're literally abusing it. You've applied under false premise. That's fraud. Literally every company that uses H1B, abuses H1B. A few years ago, my company had to renew sponsorship for one of our H1B developers. They really wanted to keep the guy (and he *was* good at his job). As part of the process, we had to post the position and then "prove" that we couldn't find anyone in the US to fill it. The job posting was created to itemize the exact skillset this developer had, and the exact job duties this developer did. Any candidate that didn't meet the requirements *exactly* was rejected. I'm not sure I'd call it "fraud" per se. Companies usually comply with the letter of the law, just not the spirit. It's all in the definition of "qualified". If a company defines "qualified" as a person who has the exact characteristics of the H1B person they want to hire (or keep), then they are keeping the letter of the law, even if not the spirit. The law cannot mandate how wide or narrow the "qualifications" must be, leaving a nice big loophole for companies to walk through. Using a loophole does not by itself constitute fraud. Because the whole process is stupid. If the company really wants to keep/hire a specific person, they shouldn't have to jump through hoops to do so. We now expect graduates to have masters degrees and experience Microsoft did not require masters degrees for entry level dev jobs. It's common for Indian/Chinese folks to _have_ masters degrees, because going to the US for graduate school is a common "first step" in the process to get their feet on US soil - F1 student visas. The way it typically works for an Indian or Chinese software engineer to get into Microsoft (or any of the majors, I assume) - get 4 year degree (real or fake) back home- get accepted to US grad school; get student VISA (F1)- do grad school- look for US company that will sponsor/supply an H1-B slot- get OPT status for STEM (up to 3 yr extension of residence permit w/ industry work authorization)- start at new company under H1-B status- start the Greencard process Now, here is what is structurally fraudulent about this. 1) The F1 visa is intended for students who plan to leave when they are done. It is not intended to be the on-ramp into the US labor force.2) the OPT extension to the F1 VISA is not intended to be a long term resident work permit. They max out at 3 years for software people3) Greencard sponsorship in this scenario is supposed to be downstream of the assertion that there is no human being already in America willing/able to do the work. From a company POVThe converion rate from OPT to H1B varies; the # of H1B lottery slots are fixed, and people who run out their OPT clock without securing an H1-B lottery slot are allegedly supposed to go home. The corps know that. So the OPT period essentially turns out to be a "no risk try out" where the company gets to see who is productive and supplicant. This continues once someone has an H1B; that person knows that the moment they lose their job, they have n days to find a different H1B slot before they are subject to a removal order. An H1B friend explained it to me: as long as he's on H1B, he never says no to working late; he never says no to taking the extra incident case, he never says no. So he wants a Greencard for safety/security reasons; so he can stop being as much of an indentured servant. The colleges love this system because F1 students can be charged full price to attend. The companies love this because they get indentured servants with a tryout period. And because they have an infinite supply of young 3rd world labor who is fighting over the scraps of available slots, which causes dramatic wage suppression. A House Candidate Says She Was Raped While Campaigning. It’s a Horrific Account—and an Urgent One.
2026-10-09

A House Candidate Says She Was Raped While Campaigning. It’s a Horrific Account—and an Urgent One.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The story Bernadette Greene-Placentia has told reporters and constituents about what happened to her on an early August morning is the exact kind of thing women are raised to fear: a stranger rape, with violent and terrifying men coming out of the darkness and attacking a lone woman. Greene-Placentia, though, isn’t an average woman. She’s running for Congress, and as she campaigns for Arizona’s 8th Congressional District Seat, she’s also speaking out about what happened to her. Doing so is brave. But it’s also a reminder of why representative democracies have to be representative. Women the world over live with the white noise of threatened sexual violence, a reality so pervasive it becomes nearly invisible. That threat shapes how we move through the world, who we trust, and where we go. And it shapes our politics. Greene-Placentia says that she was attacked while out repairing campaign signs around 2 a.m. Two men snuck up behind her, hit her over the head, and raped her. Her memory of the events, she says, are spotty—she believes she was concussed. It took her some time to even tell her own family, let alone the police. Eventually, though, she began talking about what happened. And now she’s gone public. Nearly every woman, I suspect, has had the kind of heart-pounding moment in which we fear being attacked in the way Greene-Placentia says she was. It’s the reason so many of us walk to our cars with our keys poking through our fingers, why we tense up when someone walks too closely behind us on a dark street. It’s the reason so many of us make our lives smaller, or at least more expensive: Why we pay for taxis instead of walking home, why we attend self-defense courses or forgo night shifts, why we leave parties early or skip solo trips, why we carefully plan running routes and hiking paths, why the unluckiest among us wind up with thousands of dollars in therapy bills and lost wages. One in five American women will either be raped in her lifetime or survive someone attempting to rape her. I think it’s safe to say that close to five in five will fear being raped. Young women—the group most at risk for being raped—are also the country’s most pro-choice voters. That isn’t to say that the prospect of sexual violence is the single deciding factor for making young women particularly supportive of abortion rights. But it stands to reason that the possibility of getting pregnant pushes many young women into the pro-choice camp. And realizing that many abortion opponents want to criminalize the procedure even in cases of rape—something so many women fear—no doubt influences many women’s politics. Greene-Placentia is running against MAGA incumbent Abe Hamadeh, who had, in an earlier run for Arizona attorney general, said he would prosecute abortion providers under Arizona law. At the time, that law was among the harshest in the nation and had no exceptions for rape or incest. Hamadeh’s comments were controversial, and he narrowly lost the election; Arizona’s abortion law has also since been liberalized. But Hamadeh is clear he opposes abortion. There are of course plenty of women who oppose abortion, too; some even head the nation’s most influential anti-abortion groups. But it also doesn’t seem a coincidence that as the Republican Party has become more male, it has also become much more anti-abortion. And while women have surged into elected office, it’s largely been as Democrats. Even women who embrace traditional conservative principles (small government, free markets, the family as the primary organizing unit of society, a muscular foreign policy) or MAGA conservative ones (whatever Trump says) cannot escape the reality of living in a female body, which is that even if you make all the “right” decisions, you can still be subjected to extreme male violence—and if that violence is rape, it can be compounded by a pregnancy you do not want to bear. Many of the women of today’s conservative movement, even those who once found hyper-masculine and cartoonishly misogynist MAGA antics to be funny and edgy, are coming to the realization that they are not the exception to the MAGA woman-hating rule—that even if you’re a cool girl, you’re still a woman surrounded by misogynists, and you’re the object of their hate. Ten years ago this week, a tape came out of then–presidential candidate Donald Trump boasting about grabbing women’s genitals, and then his administration went to great lengths to withhold the Epstein files, attacked abortion rights, and intervened on behalf of the Tate brothers, arguably the world’s the most famous and virulent misogynists, who are also criminally accused rapists and sex traffickers. (They deny the accusations.) It’s no wonder Trump’s support among women is tanking. Women are not inherently virtuous, and we certainly aren’t inherently liberal. The country should have more female legislators because the country’s legislatures should represent the country. But the country should also have more representative—and more female—legislatures because women really do have life experiences that are different than men’s, and that shapes how we understand politics, policy, and the law. Central to those differences are our ability to get pregnant, our physical vulnerability to crimes including rape, and the shadow of fear that follows us as a result. Greene-Placentia’s story is an experience that, heartbreakingly, millions of women have also survived—and that some haven’t. She describes an experience that nearly all women fear. That she’s speaking out about it puts one more crack in the lie that women who are raped should carry any shame. And it is another reason more women should be making the laws that shape women’s lives. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Florida Is About to Kill My Friend. I Hope the Executioners Read This First.
2026-10-09

Florida Is About to Kill My Friend. I Hope the Executioners Read This First.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Years ago I was a lawyer for William Reaves, who will soon be put to death in Florida. He gunned down a police officer in 1986. William is a murderer. He is also a war veteran with combat PTSD and polysubstance use disorder who, on the night of the crime, did only what training and trauma dictated. I got to know him well and care for him as a friend. For years I have wondered what it means to hold all of those clashing truths in my mind at once. As his execution approaches, I’m no closer to understanding. There are two stories of William’s life. In the prevailing narrative, he’s a monster who shot a good cop as the man begged for his life. In the untold story, he’s a victim of circumstance—a sweet kid from a small town who was sent to war, trained to kill in the service of his country, and returned home a psychological ruin. Both stories are true. Only the first has been given moral weight in popular memory. The second—the tale of William the war hero—explains his crime. That story should be heard before it comes to an end on Oct. 20 at 6 p.m. Eastern. That is when William will die by lethal injection. The story most people know unfolded late at night in a Zippy Mart parking lot. William was deep in a cocaine bender, a gun tucked in the waistband of his sweatpants. Unsure how to get home, he had the bright idea to call 911 from a pay phone and ask for a cab. The dispatcher sent Deputy Richard Raczkoski instead. By all accounts, Raczkoski was a good man and a good cop. On that night he was both. He did not arrest William or chase him off. He called for a ride and waited to make sure William got home safely. Everything would have been fine but for a banal twist of fate. As they waited, the gun slipped from William’s waistband and clattered to the pavement. The mishap would have been comical had it not led to Raczkoski’s death and, later this month, William’s. Both men saw the loose weapon. Both lunged for it. William got there first. Raczkoski pleaded for William not to kill him and turned to run, but William shot him four times in the back. When Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis signed the death warrant setting the execution date, he had this story, and it alone, in mind. So did the law enforcement officers who urged DeSantis to choose William for execution from more than 200 inmates on Florida’s death row. So do news outlets that affix the “cop killer” epithet to William in headlines. For most people, the story of William’s crime feels compatible with a state-run ritual to poison him to death. The other true story of his life may not. William grew up in the small community of Gifford, Florida, where folks thought he might become a pastor. He helped out at church and around the neighborhood. Family and friends remember him as a good kid, gentle and kind—not a monster in the making. The man who killed Raczkoski emerged only after circumstances radically altered the course of his life. While still a teenager, William was taken from his community and sent to Vietnam. He went on search-and-destroy missions, spending day after day under the ever-present threat of stumbling into a firefight. A friend who served beside him said they would remind each other, “Don’t half-step,” which meant to stay alert, never to let your guard down. William’s body learned to stay locked in a state of hypervigilance that eventually would not switch off. Older soldiers in Charlie Platoon—men William looked up to—trained him to use heroin and opium-laced marijuana to numb the unrelenting terror. One day as William was soft-stepping through the jungle he found himself looking down his rifle at an enemy soldier. The man was urinating on a tree with his weapon on the ground. Their eyes met, and William saw the man realize he was about to die. But William did not fire. It just felt wrong. When William’s commanding officer found out, he was furious. Never let ’em go! Get them before they get you! Kill or be killed! William learned the lesson well. He killed to survive as friends died around him. He made it home. But the constant panic and the conditioning to use drugs against it came with him. He went to a VA hospital for help but was turned away. Combat post-traumatic stress disorder was not yet understood. So William bought drugs on the street to self-medicate what the VA had not yet named. For protection while he did it, he began carrying a gun in his waistband. As one of William’s lawyers, I sat with mental health experts who evaluated him, and from them I came to understand what really happened on the night of the crime. William, they explained, was in the grip of a flashback. He felt himself again in a firefight in Vietnam and could not—not did not, but could not—govern his impulses. William’s flashbacks were triggered by men in uniform and the sight of guns. When Raczkoski, a uniformed officer, lunged for a fallen weapon, William’s combat training and mortal fear took command. He acted reflexively. Even though Raczkoski only wanted to help him, William thought he was fighting for his life. The last time William had seen a uniformed man standing over a loose weapon, he had done nothing. But that mistake had since been drilled out of him. Never let ’em go! Get them before they get you! Kill or be killed! One fragment of evidence from William’s case always struck me. A witness inside the Zippy Mart testified that after the shooting William ran in a zigzag pattern toward the tree line. No one was firing upon him. No one was around to fire upon him. His actions made no sense in context and thus proved his state of mind. Plain and simple, William thought he was back in Vietnam. Alone in the dark with no one around, his mind was in the jungle, and his body was doing what it had been trained to do when faced with the enemy. In 2017, a federal judge overturned William’s death sentence and ordered a new sentencing because William’s jury had not heard the expert testimony about William’s mental illness. The jurors who decided by a vote of 10 to 2 that William deserved to die did not know that William had PTSD or that he was reliving Vietnam when he killed Raczkoski. The federal judge acknowledged William’s heroic combat service and the tradition of extending leniency to veterans whose crimes bear the wounds of war. At a new sentencing, a jury would finally hear the whole story. But Florida appealed and the 11th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals reversed the decision—not because the testimony was false or unimportant, but because a procedural rule barred the federal court from considering evidence that had not been presented in state court. The story of William the war hero was deemed legally incognizable. People often complain that defendants escape punishment because clever lawyers get them off on a technicality. William, however, will be executed on one. Murders are often called “senseless.” I understand why. They seem so until someone does the difficult work of presenting the evidence that traces fate’s path from causes to consequence. The truth is there are always reasons. Maybe not good ones, not comforting ones, not ones that lessen the tragedy or grief, and sometimes not ones we can make visible, but there are always reasons. William did what he did for reasons. Those reasons were unknown to his jury. But Florida is going to execute him anyway. I have come to understand that moral outrage abhors understanding. People experience attempts to explain murder as an affront to the victim. They feel that admitting a murder happened for reasons would somehow diminish or dishonor its tragedy. They prefer to blame a monster without history or humanity. To satisfy their need to condemn someone—rather than circumstances, a war, a mental disorder, a tragic coincidence—murder cannot be allowed to make sense. People ask of William’s crime, How could he do something like that? When I reflect on the events of his life, I ask, Is it any wonder that he did? The trauma, the training, the drugs, the incident with the enemy solider all culminated at the Zippy Mart as if the world had arranged itself around William to preordain his actions. When I represented William, we talked about his life. I kept greeting cards from him and his family thanking me for the work I did on his case, even though we lost. In one he wrote, “I LOVE YOU MAN!!” and I treasure that one, because I have love in my heart for William. I think of him as a friend. At the same time, I know he murdered a good man and officer in a horrific tragedy. For me, both are true. For me, William is not reducible to Cop Killer or, for that matter, War Hero, not one or the other. He is both and more—a broken man of complex humanity and contradictions, vulnerable like the rest of us to what the Supreme Court has called in its death penalty jurisprudence “the diverse frailties of humankind.” William’s execution will belong entirely to its time. We live in an age of dichotomous thinking, with social-media algorithms feeding rival camps differing facts, constructing differing worlds of intersubjective reality. Nuance is weakness. We are losing what capacity we had to hold the tension of opposites, to think complexly about a complex world. And so William must be a cop killer only, not also a war hero, not more. For my friend William, I hold the tension of opposites. I engage confusion. I remain with complexity long enough for irreconcilable surface truths to give way to deeper meanings and difficult understanding. The Eighth Amendment prohibits cruel and unusual punishments, which the Supreme Court defines as those that violate the “evolving standards of decency that mark the progress of a maturing society.” Justice Thurgood Marshall argued that most people do not know enough about the death penalty—how it operates, the limits of its reliability, how often it fails—to make an informed judgment about whether it is decent. Some of us view uninformed decency as none at all. William’s story is a case study in the death penalty’s indecency. But Marshall was right. No one sees it, because no one wants to sit with the duality and moral vagaries of William’s life stories long enough for understanding to emerge. Since the moment I heard William will be killed on Oct. 20, I’ve been on edge. I feel guilty for losing his case and, for some reason, scared, as if I along with him am unsafe. I think it has something to do with another unseen outcome of the death penalty—secondary trauma. Years after I represented William, a psychiatrist surprised me by saying we should discuss my work-related PTSD. A lot clicked into place in that moment. I was aware of the studies showing secondary traumatic stress in criminal defense lawyers and, even more so, in capital defense lawyers. We are known to develop PTSD-like symptoms from repeated exposure to the traumas of death work. For instance, we may experience hyperarousal and intrusive recollections. I keep replaying arguments with William’s prosecutors—colleagues who dedicated their careers to killing my friend. They only let themselves see the cop killer. I keep thinking that I share something with William, a trauma derivative of his. I keep checking and rechecking his execution date and time on my Outlook calendar, right there among the work meetings and menial tasks. I teach at a law school now. On the night of Oct. 20 at 6 p.m., I happen to have a class. When they strap William to a gurney and inject him with poison in front of an audience of spectators, I’ll be with a promising group of students who believe in the law. I’ll be teaching them that the law seeks truth, upholds reason, and does justice, while wondering if the state has stopped my friend’s heart yet. I’ll wonder how I can teach faith in the law at the very hour the law fails my friend by ending his life. And once again, I will have no choice but to hold clashing truths together in my mind. The Latest Example of Trumpworld’s Corruption Is Pretty Funny. It’s Also Really Bad.
2026-10-08

The Latest Example of Trumpworld’s Corruption Is Pretty Funny. It’s Also Really Bad.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Blatant corruption by Donald Trump and his family gets a lot of attention, and rightly so. In recent weeks, we’ve learned that a Russian oligarch helped pay for Donald Trump Jr.’s tropical wedding (Trump Jr. paid him back after the story broke) and that U.S. talks to end the war in Ukraine reportedly include an oil deal that would enrich investors in Trump family ventures. But focusing only on the first family can overlook similarly sleazy episodes of grift involving far smaller figures in the president’s orbit. In fact, it’s starting to look like many Trumpworld bit players, including some who don’t work for him directly, have been monetizing their ties to the administration. Let’s take a wade into the ever-deepening swamp. Which Trump hangers-on are we talking about and what did they allegedly do? At least one of them is a current administration official. This week, the Wall Street Journal reported that Kimberly Guilfoyle, Trump’s ambassador to Greece (and Don Jr.’s ex-fiancée), had begged a Trump donor to pay $100,000 of her American Express bill last year in exchange for getting Trump’s IRS commissioner to resolve the donor’s tax issues. (Ultimately, the donor never paid.) Officials who have since left office have also been accused of behavior that stinks of self-dealing. As Trump’s Homeland Security secretary, Kristi Noem shot a taxpayer-funded ad campaign reportedly run by a consulting firm linked to her and her aides. And while serving in government, Noem adviser Corey Lewandowski reportedly tried to persuade Qatar and the United Arab Emirates to hire him on the side. Yikes. You mentioned people who don’t work for the government getting in on the grift, too? Yep. The New York Times reported this week that James Blair and Chris LaCivita, two of Trump’s top political advisers, got hundreds of thousands of dollars for advising the Russia-aligned government of a small Balkan territory. And as the Financial Times revealed today, it turns out that the daughter of Trump’s chief of staff works at a lobbying firm that’s done work for the same government. Have any of these people faced consequences? Some have, and others might. Gabriel Perez, an aide who operated Trump’s teleprompter, recently left the administration after revelations that he placed lucrative bets on the prediction market Kalshi based on the content of the president’s speeches. Guilfoyle’s days in the administration may be numbered, too. The Daily Mail reports that the State Department is investigating her over possible substance abuse, improperly accepting gifts, and more. Trump, meanwhile, seems mad at Blair and LaCivita, threatening to fire them over their Balkans work “if there’s a conflict.” But isn’t a lot of this lesser corruption … kind of Trump’s fault? It’s fair to think so. The president has established a culture of impunity from the top. His administration has fired inspectors general tasked with investigating federal corruption and seems to have intervened to help allies caught red-handed. In 2024, future “border czar” Tom Homan was reportedly recorded taking $50,000 in cash from undercover FBI agents. But Homan denied wrongdoing and the Justice Department closed the case after Trump took office. OK, that doesn’t look great. But don’t Democrats do this too? What about Hunter Biden? Fair question! Hunter was never a member of either the Obama or Biden administration, but he does seem to have traded on his last name to get money from a Ukrainian gas company, among other things. Still, if you think it’s a problem when Democrats leverage political connections for financial gain, you should probably be outraged about what Trump’s minions are doing, too. To some extent, these are the consequences of lowered expectations. When a president openly commingles his personal interests with government work, it creates a permission structure for people below him to act in similar ways. But the conduct of this particular president’s underlings may end up embedding a culture of corruption that lasts beyond him. Many of them, like their boss, evidently don’t believe they’ll face serious accountability for their behavior. And although most federal employees don’t exploit their public roles for private gain, most Americans already believe government corruption is endemic. Realizing the public’s worst fears can fuel apathy about and disgust with politics, which in turn can help corrupt people get away with it. For that reason, the danger may not end when Trump leaves office. Instead, he and his underlings may be creating a precedent for future administrations that corruption isn’t just tolerated, it’s expected. OK, time to pivot from the slow debasement of public service to a public-service announcement from my colleagues about what to read and listen to if you’re looking for a distraction: A modern couple’s dilemma: In the latest installment of Unhinged, Steffi Cao counsels a letter writer about his ex-girlfriend getting upset when he made social plans without her—and whether it’s a red flag if the person you’re dating doesn’t have friends of their own. Some love for the runners-up: In the 1980s, the pop charts were full of near-miss hits, in which songs stalled out just before reaching No. 1 or making it into the Top 10 or Top 40. Tune in to the newest episode of Hit Parade to learn why the Reagan era was chock-full of iconic No. 2, No. 11, and No. 41 hits. A dark read: In 1977, Stephen King pseudonymously published Rage, an unsettling novel about a school shooter. Jack Hamilton explores how this controversial story connects to King’s better-known works. Some tips for scaredy cats: If you don’t like spooky scary skeletons, creepy crawlies, or horror flicks, Halloween season can be a fright to endure. But there are ways to conquer your fears. Mathias Clasen, the author of A Very Nervous Person’s Guide to Horror Movies, explains how jumpscares work, and how you can learn to love them—or at least tolerate them. Spooky season may be in full swing, but never fear: I’ll be back in your inbox tomorrow. Enjoy your night! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
